# Done

Finished backlog items, recent first, one table per lane so that lanes
finishing at the same time don't edit the same lines. A lane writes only
in its own table, whatever the row's letter: an L row the Editor lane
finishes goes in the Editor table (2026-11-05, after the 10-hour run's
rebases kept conflicting where two lanes wrote the same table). QA,
reviews, rounds and the coordinator's rows go in the last table. Open work is in
[BACKLOG.md](../../BACKLOG.md); how an item moves here is in
[WORKFLOW.md](WORKFLOW.md) ("Doing an item"). Before 2026-10-30 these
tables were the Done section of BACKLOG.md; older history is in git and
in docs/history/ROADMAP.md's Done table.

| C58 (step j) | In C, `array of` a table's keys (swarm's `keys of` each tick) fills the array from the table's slots, sorted as the entries are, with no list made to be copied in and freed: allocating, freeing and walking that list was about a fifth of swarm's native run (callgrind). c.ts, beside C207b's `array of` a range. swarm native 0.39 to 0.35 s, same answers. Test: compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts (swarm natively) | this PR |
| C58 (step i) | C tables grow at half used, removed slots counted, not 3/4, and move into twice the room unless at most a quarter is full: swarm's motes come and go each tick, leaving removed slots each lookup walked past. swarm native 0.44 to 0.39 s (best of 5), same answers. Tests: compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts, c-cases.test.ts | this PR |
| C58 (step h) | swarm native: listing a C table's entries sorted its slots with qsort and a compare per pair, a third of the run (callgrind), as `keys of` runs each tick. c.ts tableC now sorts Int keys that are all small and within 2^32 of each other by a radix sort of (key − least, slot) words, qsort otherwise. swarm native 1.05 to about 0.55 s, same answers. Test: compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts (C, 151 keys) | this PR |
| C58 (step g) | swarm's `moved` walks the keys by position: compiler/src/array-offsets.ts (C196) took a walk by first, length and rest only where the array was read under its own name, and `moved`'s match names it again (`let l2 = l0`). Another name for the walked array is now the same walk. Each step was a view made for the rest, and past three quarters of the buffer a copy. swarm 0.95 to 0.72 s compiled, same answers (compiler/test/examples-agree.test.ts) | this PR |
| C58 (step f) | `keys of` on a hash table straight from it: std's `table keys list` (TABLE_OPS.keys), lowered as a list of the table's entries whose items are its keys, as a set's items already were (scalar.ts `keyList`; C and WebAssembly print such a list of keys). On JavaScript, Int keys put in order are one array under the list (bytes.ts ItemsList), which `array of` reads in place. Before, each tick made a pair per mote, mapped them to keys, and copied the list into an array. swarm 1.2 to 0.95 s compiled, 1.25 to 1.05 s native, same answers on JS, C and WebAssembly. Test: compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts lists the keys on every target | this PR |
| C58 (step e) | Defaults first, first cost: in compiled JavaScript a put or remove on a hash table nobody else holds is written in place. std's Table kept every version a value (C71), so each change made a new version and an undo record, about half of swarm's run in the collector. compiler/src/owned-tables.ts asks one-holder.ts, from the program, whether every table and hash set has one holder (the hash table rows' jobs read as they do them, put, remove, merge and union changing their first table); then their changes are std.ts `changeOwned`, which writes into the table it was given. swarm 2.6 s to 1.4 s compiled, same answers; a map whose old version is read again keeps the versions. Test: compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts | this PR |
| C58 (step d) | swarm on a Pool (ters/examples/swarm-pool): the same answers as the Map version, 1.5 to 1.8 s compiled against 2.6 s, and 0.82 s native against 1.35 s. At first it took 190 s, because the chooser stores lists as arrays only where none sits inside a library type, so the Pool's slots stayed cons lists. ters/src/lang/own-holders.ts now copies plain library data types holding lists (Pool, Queue) and the library definitions using them into the program, so their lists are the program's own (cli/represented.ts). Also found: `append([key], recent)` made the chooser pick trees for the whole program, `cons` arrays. test/pools.test.ts: swarm-pool stores every list as an array and compiled matches evaluated. | this PR |
| C58 (step c) | Pool, a collection that gives out its own keys (std.ts Pool and Pool key, prelude: empty pool, next key, added to pool, pool item, replaced in pool, removed from pool, pool keys, pool size). Items sit in slots with each slot's generation; a removed item's slot goes on the free list and is reused by the next one added, and its generation goes up, so a key kept from before finds nothing rather than the new item. `next key` is the key the next add gives, so an item can hold its own. Written in the language over lists, which the compiler stores as arrays (item at and replace at constant). test/pools.test.ts: evaluated and compiled agree. | this PR |

Drags too: tumble had none, on the web either; a finger dragged from a place into its neighbour swaps them (tumble's dragged and toward; touch-action: none on the board, and on Android a box that hears pointermove keeps the finger from the scroll view). The headless screen takes frames:N:MS and pointerdown:ID:X,Y. c-page.test.ts plays the hint's swap, by taps and by a drag, through clear, fall and refill to rest; the drag checked in Chromium with touch too. | this PR |

Notebook with 300 notes, in process: 2.55 to 2.0 s (median of 10 on a loaded machine), the same output | this PR |

| L118e | In the direct run (L118c), a ref to a definition or a builtin isn't looked for in the scope, only binders are there (a scope given from outside through `ev` with anything else turns this off), and its value is kept at the ref: missing it walked the whole scope, most of agent's own time. agent's calls in 20 s: 17.2 to 19.2 million (+12%); notebook with 300 notes 1.45 to 1.36 s | this PR |
| E103q | A palette offer with a bare use in it is filled in the shared copy of its base's filled nodes (ports.ts filledFrom's mirror, as the offers without one already were), not in a copy of all 43k nodes, and fillUses lists what it writes instead of a scan of every node for what changed. Fall's 17 holes: 9.9 to 8.75 s, the same offers | this PR |
| L118c | The evaluator runs functions that only compute as JavaScript compiled once per node, falling back to the heap machine where a search, a handler or the world needs it (L118c2 #2154, L118c3 #2159, L118c4 #2161; first step: `ters run` through the core IR). Notebook with 300 notes, in process: 4.65 to 1.85 s. Functions with a choice are left to L118d | this PR |
| I192b | A runner copies the program's nodes only when a derivative first writes into them (run.ts `writable`), not each time one is made: copying was 20 ms of every makeRunner, and opening a definition makes several (its values, main's run, the backward search). makeRunner on constructions 22 to 0.5 ms. Opening (click to frame, twice each): constructions `is picked` 0.36 to 0.26 s, calc `blank` 0.28 to 0.22 s, break `shifted` 0.17 to 0.15 s. With L118c2-c4 (measured before and after them): Spacetime `scalar part`'s views 0.40 to 0.25 s, ray marcher `shadow`'s tasks 0.35 to 0.28 s | this PR |
| L118c4 | The direct run (L118c2) makes each node once into a JavaScript function of its scope (closure compilation, run.ts compileNode), made as it is first reached: no node lookup or switch on its kind per run. Notebook with 300 notes, in process: 2.36 to 1.85 s (median of 10). Together with L118c2 and L118c3: 4.65 to 1.85 s. Tried and set aside again: functions with a choice tried directly, finishing a stopped one on a machine of its own; 4.2 s, slower | this PR |
| L118c3 | A closure carries what the evaluator runs it by (its lambda's node and scope) on its META object, set as a property, instead of a WeakMap entry and an Object.assign per closure made: making closures was the run's second cost after L118c2. Notebook with 300 notes, in process: 3.5 to 2.95 s (median of 10). Tried and set aside: running functions with a choice in their type directly as well; json.test hung, every stop deep in a recursive descent reran the whole outer call | this PR |
| L118c2 | The evaluator runs a function whose type says it only computes (no effect but diverging) as plain JavaScript on the JavaScript stack, not step by step on the heap machine (run.ts `direct`): calls, lets, matches, constructors, fields and the builtins that only compute; `first`, `all`, a fold and the rest run on a machine of their own inside it. The moment a run would need the machine (an operation, a handler, a builtin that acts on the world, no value or several, the stack running out) it stops, having done nothing anyone sees, and the call starts again on the machine; a function that keeps stopping stays there. Notebook with 300 notes, in process: 4.65 to 3.55 s (median of 10), machine calls 547k to 268k; Spacetime's charge path 511 to 409 ms. Left: functions with a choice in their type (176k of the notebook's calls) still run on the machine | this PR |

Time stays in every row (Ersagun: reading the clock isn't pure); Abort and Check are never a host's. In the evaluator (run.ts `unhandled`, hosts.ts `stdHosted`), so `lab run` and `lab test` with hosts; FOREIGN.md says so. Example: test/hosts.test.ts's main prints 1234 from the host's clock and 5 from the world's | #2012 |

(e) done: steps over operations answered now (L229e): an operation is answered later where it is declared so or where any case takes its rest (later-ops.ts `laterOps`, read by infer, run, compile.ts, handlers.ts and scalar.ts), and the core IR's later handlers answer at once for a case that leaves the rest out (L63). | #1853 |

| L211 | A use gives its produced ports as a tuple (decided in L179, PORTS-2026-11-01.md §3 "Decided (L179)"). Step a done: a use with no body is read at load (ports.ts `fillUses`, from `base` and `fromFile`) as the use binding each produced port with their tuple as its body, so every engine runs it. Step b done: the model's `binds` and `body` are optional, read through model.ts `bindsOf`/`bodyOf` (compiler/src/uses.ts since C158, the editor since E240). Step c done: the checker types a use with no body as its produced port or their tuple (infer.ts `useResult`), each port typed where a binder would be, with Choice as before. Step d done: the evaluator runs one (run.ts, ports.ts `answerKeys`; the passes on its way read model.ts `useKids`). Step e done: the modes analysis and the relational interpreter read one with its ports bound (ports.ts `filled`, a copy made once per program). Step f done: derive.ts copies one, and the renderers show one as a call naming its given ports (`add(… = 2, result = 5)`, sexpr `(solve add (…))`). Step g done: saved uses move with a `SHAPE_STEPS` entry (ports.ts `usesGiveTheirPorts`): no body, the old body a let of the value, a binder's read a part of it. The compilers run it (C161, #1429). Step h done: a load keeps it as it is (`fillUses` only in ports.ts `filled`). Step i done: the DSL's `use` and `solve` build today's shape (the library is written so), and a tuple's part of a use reads by its port's name (`tuple2.length`). Left: `binds`/`body` leave the model once the editor writes no use with them (Editor lane). A use binding a port with a unit moves too, the binder kept and bound by a let of its part (L211j). The editor's drawing done (each produced port an output, wired to what reads it). Step k done: the editor writes a use with no binds or body (the palette's use, refactor.ts). Steps of about an hour (2026-11-05): (l) model.ts's call loses `binds` and `body`, bindsOf/bodyOf read none; (m) a SHAPE_STEPS entry drops any left on load. **(l) measured 2026-11-05 09:05:** taking the two fields off the model's use gives 86 type errors in 21 files, and not only readers: the passes after a program is filled write uses with binds and a body themselves (results-fn.ts `use`, compiler/src/inverses.ts's two, arith.ts's and directions.ts's port renames, represent.ts's and arith.ts's copies, infer.ts 2297's directions check), and compile.ts and scalar.ts fill (`withBodies`) before resolving, so a bare use written by resolution would reach them unfilled. So (l) is about three hours, split: (l1) done: a `Bound` use type (model.ts: a use with binds and body, made only by ports.ts `fillUses` and the passes above), `Use` without them; the readers that take either (hash.ts, units.ts, spine.ts, one-holder.ts, render/nested.ts, render/sexpr.ts, render.ts, model.ts's id map, derive.ts's copy) read through `isBound`; (l2) done: the passes above take and write `Bound`, or write a bare use and fill it where they make it (results-fn's `use` is a bare use already: its body is its produced ports' tuple); (l3) done: the model's use with no ports bound has no `binds` or `body` in its type; a use has both (`Bound`) or neither. (m) dropped (2026-11-05): after load nothing in the repo (47 saved programs and library files) or the library keeps a bound use; one left in a user's save is a use whose ports aren't known, and dropping its binds and body would lose what the user wrote. `useNode` is gone: no one builds a bound use but the DSL, which moves it at once (`bare`, the prelude byte for byte the same), derive.ts's and the editor's copies of a kept one, and the passes after `filled`. | #1789 |

| L88e3 | Comparisons at Real that narrow until they decide: `real less than(a, b, fuel)` works a − b out to 2⁻⁰, 2⁻⁸, 2⁻¹⁶, … until it is clear of 0, and has no answer (Choice) once it is within 2⁻ᶠᵘᵉˡ and still undecided, which equal Reals always are (real.ts `compare`). π < 22/7 is true; 1/3 · 3 < 1 has none. Test: real.test.ts (two Reals 2⁻¹⁰⁰ apart: undecided with 64 bits, decided with 128; equal ones never; evaluated and compiled) | #1768 |
| L88e2 | π and digits at Real: `real pi()` is π, worked out to 2⁻ᵖ by Machin's formula (16·atan(1/5) − 4·atan(1/239), its series in Ints with guard bits), the same value wherever it's made, so what it worked out is kept; `real digits(x, n)` is x rounded to n decimals, right within one in the last place (whether a Real is just under or over a decimal can't always be told, so it rounds rather than cuts). 2000 decimals of π in 4 ms. Test: real.test.ts (π to 50 decimals, 200 agreeing with 260 rounded, 2π evaluated and compiled) | #1765 |
| L88e1 | `Real` in std (EXACT-REALS.md step 4): a number held by how it was made, a fraction or + − · of Reals, that gives `approximate(x, p)`, an Int m with |x − m/2ᵖ| < 2⁻ᵖ, for any p asked. Each operation asks its parts for just the precision it needs (Boehm's bounds) and keeps what it worked out, so asking again or narrower reuses it (real.ts). The one + − · and a number written at Real (`arithmetic at real`, `literal at real`); evaluated and compiled to JavaScript. Test: real.test.ts (500 random + − · expressions at five precisions, exact against their fractions) | #1759 |

| L210d | steady atan, atan2, asin and acos: fdlibm's s_atan.c, e_atan2.c, e_asin.c and e_acos.c in the steady module. atan2's k, (high word of y − high word of x) >> 20, is worked out from the high words as whole numbers; with one zero, atan2(0, x) for a negative x is π. Outside −1 to 1 asin and acos have no answer, as √(1 − x²) has none (a partial function, not a search). The backward search checked the whole program again before its time limit applied, and so did a search a running program starts: the editor's check (and the runner's) is now handed in (relate.ts Options `info`). And main's long run for a seed ran before the definition drew: a run that isn't known to be quick now runs just after (main.ts `firstCall`). A frame is now drawn a few rows at a time, at most ~10 ms at once, and a slow one leaves the page as much time again before the next (editor/picture.ts). Measured in headless Chromium: opening `sphere` or `colour` 2.0 to 2.8 s → 0.1 to 0.7 s, and no long tasks over 3 s (21 before, 140 to 163 ms each). Opening the program itself is still ~7.6 s, the lowering's | #1076 |
| E195 | **Bug.** Typed `[1.0, 2.0]` was a `List a where a is a Number` and printed `[1, 2]`: the list was read whole as a value, which forgets the points. A value with a whole number written with a point (or an exponent) inside is now read part by part, so each is a Float64 literal (E192) and prints `1.0`; `some(2.0)` is an `Optional Float64` the same way. Test: reader.test.ts | #1073 |
| E193 | **Bug.** Undo of *+ New definition* opened the library's `Bool` (the first of the code's definitions), with nothing marked, and Redo stayed there. Each version of the program now remembers the definition open with it, the one open before the edit's own step changed it, so Undo goes back to `insert` and Redo to the new `k`; a definition Undo removed falls back to `main`. Test: editing.test.ts `undoNewDefinition` | #1071 |
| E166b | The text side of E166: a list written in place as a definition's one input reads as that call's inputs, `sum(1, 2, 3)` for `sum([1, 2, 3])`, and `p(named(…), words(" "))` for `p([…])` on several lines. The open question (it reads like a call of three inputs) is settled by the function's type, not a mark: only a definition whose one input is a List, with two or more items written in place, and whose name means nothing else, so the reader can read it back. It reads `sum(1, 2, 3)` as the list when nothing of that name takes three inputs and every meaning takes one (render.ts `spread`, typed.ts). One item keeps its brackets, `h1([words(…)])`, and so do names with other meanings (`length([1, 2])`). Prior art: Lisp's and JavaScript's rest parameters (`&rest`, `...xs`) read the same way. Test: blocks.test.ts | #1065 |
| C90 (editor half) | Opening a picture lowered it for the GPU try, made its Float32 copy and lowered that for the CPU side every time, and again when the program was opened again. Both are kept per version of the code and of what its names say (editor/picture.ts `once`), so reopening lowers nothing: editing.test's shaderOpens 21 → 15 s. The C90 row stays for staging's own check | #1061 |
| E186b | Closed with a test, no change: a stub's callers already choose a shared name's meaning. With a caller passing a Text, `length(w)` is Text's; with one passing a list, List's (a reading that breaks a caller is refused, so the next is tried). Only with no caller yet does it fall to the first meaning, List's, and nothing there says which. Test: reader.test.ts | #1060 |
| E192 | `1500.0` typed was any number (`a where a is a Number`): a whole number written with a point is now a Float64 literal, and it prints with its point (`1500.0`) so it reads back the same (typed.ts, render.ts). The other half, case analysis offering only the old type after *Clear it* and *Let it loosen*, isn't the editor's doing: the definition is in a recursive group with its callers, and inside one group a caller fixes an input's type (no polymorphic recursion), so the palette offers what checks. Reproduced in Node: g(q) and h calling g(card(…)) give q : Card. Test: reader.test.ts | #1057 |
| E191 | `≠` and `!=` weren't read ("there's no field named id ≠ id"). They now read as `not(a = b)`, since the language has no ≠ of its own (L161). A field read followed by text nothing reads now says what's left over ("after .head, “<> b” isn't read") instead of taking the rest of the line as the field's name. Test: reader.test.ts | #1055 |
| E190 | Typed text had no sequence of actions: `write(a, x); write(b, y)` was refused. A step with no name is now read as one whose value is named `_` and read by nothing, the way E183 prints it. A function whose whole body is one action, `(e) => write(a, x)`, now offers *Add a step before this…* (typed.ts `addActionStep`, `performs`). Test: reader.test.ts | #1053 |
| E188 | Typed names that read as something else. A program's own case `done` lost to std's `done(value)`: cases of one name are now told apart by how many values they take (values.ts). `random()` read as the effect's operation taken as a value, so `random() · n` was refused: an effect's operation is a call, never a value. Typing `unit` put `unit(□)` (vectors' normalize) first and Enter took it: a value named exactly what's typed now comes before a call of a function of that name, while a function still comes after its call (`random()` before `random`). Test: reader.test.ts | #1052 |
| E189 | A fresh match's cases after the first fill read as one `otherwise → □`, so a case had no hole of its own and its fields were refused. With no stored otherwise, cases whose body is still a hole never fold together (patterns.ts `sharedOtherwise`): each keeps its own hole in the text, as the graph draws it. Clearing a shared value now leaves each case its own hole too (editing.test's sharedRow says so). Test: own-case.test.ts | #1049 |
| E186 | A field read on an input nothing had settled took the first type with that field, silently (`q.title` became Card's where Quick was meant, and the refusal came two definitions later). When more than one type's reading fits, the palette now offers each, "read as a Card" / "read as a Quick", under "More than one type has these fields, and nothing here says which: pick one" (typed.ts `fieldOwners`, fillValue's `choices`; reader test). The other half, a name in a stub taking List's `length` though its one caller passes a Text, is E186b | #1047 |
| E187 | A value definition named in typed text inside a list or a constructor (`[styles, …]`, `some(save name)`, `state(starting board, …)`) was refused with infer's "points at nothing". The text read as a value, with the definition in it as a value standing for a definition (how the Run panel passes one, #703), which a node can't hold. A value that names a definition is now read as code: a reference (typed.ts; reader test) | #1044 |
| E185 | Typed `>`, `≥`, `≤` (and `>=`, `<=`) read as the prelude's hidden Float64-only `more than`, `at least`, `at most`, so `length(w) > 1` was refused. The notation (styles/blocks.json) named only the hidden copies: the generic ones now have the glyphs too, and a hidden definition is never read from typed text (typed.ts). The generic ones also print as `>`, `≥`, `≤` now | #1043 |
| I145 | The palette offered two `range`s, at Float64 and at Int, with the Float64 one first. Positions are Ints now, so of two meanings of one name in a group the Int one comes first (`range(□: Int)`, then `range(□: Float64)`). Asked by the Language lane | #1035 |
| I29 | Definitions that open with an empty Run. Last step, I29g, after a recount of converter, dice, calculus, Mandelbrot and pedal (21 of 60 still empty): a picture's pixel can miss what its helpers do (Mandelbrot's is in the set, so its palette never ran), so a helper something calls that no pixel reached is looked for at two more; a page's sound player (`listen`) plays a block of 64 samples of a sine, which reaches the pedal's helpers; a definition whose name has other marks (`as °C`, `as km/h`) reads back from a port's box (values.ts), so converter's `field` opens filled. Now 9 of 60 empty, each one nothing calls (calculus's derivatives and `x² − 2`, the pedal's `peak`): no call to borrow values from. Steps before: #578, #703, #761, #845, #900, I29e #1006, I29f #1009 | #1032 |
| I144 | Std's Pair became Tuple2 (L139), and `lab` and Open read old ids as today's, but the editor's own restore from the browser (`onToday`) didn't: a changed program that used Pair named ids that were gone, failed the check that everything resolves, and was silently replaced by the shipped example, edits lost. `onToday` now runs `onTodaysIds` on the code and names first (checked: an edited calculus saved with `std.pair…` ids keeps its edit) | #1023 |
| I137 | Make it a function on `fall`'s `+` put the new `part =` dot past the result, its wire looping back into the call. It now goes with the kept node nearest it in the fresh layout: the dot lands in the column of lets beside `drift =` and `drop =` | #1022 |
| I138 | A dot holding a function (`part : (Float64, Float64) -> Float64`, made by Make it a function) offered "Unit · part of its type" with m, s, kg… An input or dot now takes a unit only when it is a number or nothing says what it is yet | #1021 |
| I142 | Typed `match s` was refused as "nothing is named match s here". | I141 | The palette led with rows for writing representations: a new definition's calls started with `extended less`, `extended add`, …, and typing `match` listed `match by` and `match as written` above case analysis. How often a row is used now counts only the program's own definitions, not the library's (std's representations call those hundreds of times), and representation rows (`match by`, `as written`, `job`, …) sit in a last group, "for representations" | #1018 |
| I140 | `first([1], else 2)` typed was refused with "first works only on a Number (Int, Rational, …)", which reads as though another meaning of `first` were meant. It now looks through an action to what it gives, and says so at the number: "first needs List a here, and a number isn't one" (infer.ts, two lines) | #1017 |
| I143 | *Make a definition named X* (E180) named inputs it couldn't name from the call `input 1`, `input 2`, which read as `‹input 1›` everywhere. They're `in`, or `in1`, `in2` when there are several, as *Make a function* names them | #1016 |
| I136 | + New definition took 1.5 s (physics) to 2.8 s (Constructions) from Enter to drawn. Physics 2.1 → 0.7 s, Constructions 1.8 → 1.0 s, measured from the click on Create | #1015 |
| I135 | Each key typed in the palette cost 60 to 180 ms in big programs (E173b's per-key redraw): every filling was labelled again (a type shown for each), the dot handles walked every node for what's read, and *↑ what it's in* (E183b) walked them once per step up and read every node the views draw. Labels are kept per filling while the names stay, what's read and each node's parent are found once per version of the code, and only the few candidates are looked up in the views. Physics 140 → about 28 ms a key, Spacetime 200 → about 30 (the first key of a palette still labels everything, 70 to 85 ms). Also: E183b's button sat inside the Selected panel's head, so the head read "Hole↑ what it's in" and two browser tests (heldSeed, listItems) failed on main; it's a row of its own under the head now | #1012 |
| I134 | **Bug.** Inside a case of a match, *↑ what it's in* and Alt+↑ (E183b) did nothing: `parentOf` knows an arm's body, not the match or fold holding the arm, so the walk stopped there. `enclosing` now takes that step itself; from `more` in Conjugation's `bands`: `bands(more)`, the `cons(…)`, then the match. | E176 | A typed line of `++` with shows and field reads (sheet's `"line " ++ show(e.line) ++ ": " ++ e.name ++ " is " ++ cell text(results, e.name)`) was refused though each part went in alone: of its 128 combinations of meanings (`line` a field of two types, `name` of four, `show` four meanings) only the first 16 were tried. | E177 | A fresh `match x · T` read as `otherwise → □` in the text: one hole standing for every case, the first fill made in all of them, the cases' binders hidden until *Give … its own body*, while the graph drew a hole per case (sheet split 96 cases by hand). | I125 | Nine of Bounce's helpers opened on a failed search, their wires saying "a Float64 that isn't finite reached arithmetic". When main's run entered a copy and the definition as written fails on those values, the wire values (trace.ts) now try its copies and show the one that runs on the nodes it copies (97 of `moved`'s 100 nodes). | I124 | Pig's `hold at` and `quietly` opened on a failed search, their wires saying "nothing handles np2oerr2…". Such a definition is no longer run or searched on its own; its panel says "It handles Decide for the function it's given…: Run main to see it at work". The runner's "nothing handles" names the operation | this PR |

| T15 (fixes) | What the first finished Check main and the Nightly found red on main. (1) The grapheme tables were read off Unicode 16, and CI's Node 22 now has 17: they are read off 17 again (Node 22.23.4). The test now asks that they are no older than the host, and compares breaks with the host when the versions match, since an older Node has no say on characters it doesn't know. (2) #2524 kept the first of any search that used something outside it that may fail, so pond's searches kept theirs over every builtin (divide) and field, and "weight of" no longer ran on int64s on C. Now only a binder, a function it was given, keeps it (first-arms.ts) | #2548 |
| T15 (step) | Check main in six parallel jobs: a job of its own finds the base, the last run that finished, as before. Six then each run a share of the files the merge reaches (`affected.ts <base> HEAD --full --shard i/6`), balanced by nightly-times.json, as the Nightly is (I208). In one job a merge that reached most files ran past the 20-minute timeout: 94 of its last 100 runs were cancelled, so the base never moved. TESTING.md T1 says so | #2532 |
| I222 | Text as views on WebAssembly and C: reading back 100,000 numbers (backwards' read numbers) now takes 1.9 s on WASM, where it ran out of memory, and 0.6 s on C, printing what `ters run` prints (20,000: WASM 10 s → 0.4 s, C 4.8 s → 0.2 s). A Text on WASM is a `$text` (its `$bytes` array, start, size, characters counted once, fill), and a slice of 64 bytes or more is a view on the same array, as on C (#2522); length, offset, slice, find, equal, less and code work on the view, and the rest of the runtime runs its old form on a plain array through `text.raw`/`text.of`. A number read from a text fails at its first byte that can't be in one, without copying a long rest first. And a ++ b of 64 bytes or more goes into an array with room on the shorter side, so a text built a piece at a time (show numbers) grows into that room instead of copying everything before each piece: C at 300,000 numbers 9.6 s → 2.1 s. Test: solved-c's hundred thousand numbers | #2528 |
| C217 | A page's sound in the native apps: the C page keeps each `listen` and plays its block function on the platform's audio thread (AAudio on Android, AVAudioEngine on iOS), 128 samples at a time at the device's rate with the page's own cells, the two threads taking turns under one page lock (streams stopped and nodes detached off it); the recording ships in the app and is read as WAV in C, the microphone is asked for. Each second the app logs loudness in and out and how much the sound changed what came in. Pedal's A strum on the Android emulator (44100 a second) and the iPhone simulator (48000): driven at 32 it changes the loudest second by 0.022, at 128 by 0.031, Clean by 0.0000, loudness kept the same. The CI Mac has no microphone, which the page says. Also: spacetime builds natively now (test updated). C195 checked after #2515: backwards and conjugate print on C what `ters run` prints | #2519 |

Since C207b `array of` a range is filled at once and dropping a count is inline (divisors 0.87 to 0.45 s; hand-written C 0.092 s). Since C207c a fold of a map is one fold (fuse-steps.ts; divisors 0.42 to 0.24 s, nicomachus 0.43 to 0.049 s). Since C207d a fold over `array of` a range counts down with no array (divisors 0.24 to 0.155 s, 1.7x hand-written C). Since C207e WebAssembly's Int add, sub, equal and less are inline while both are i31s (divisors 0.47 to 0.21 s, collatz 1.88 to 1.48 s under Node's WASI). Since C207f a fold to a function applied at once (`fold xs from d = …`) is a forward loop (queens-count 0.25 to 0.12 s; hand-written C 0.03 s; its search, the choice and the placed list made per step, is the rest of its gap). Since C207e on C a loop's counting Ints are int64_ts (untagged-ints.ts; divisors 0.19 to 0.14 s, nicomachus 31 to 12 ms). Since C207g an array's item is read on C with the array lent (queens-first 7.7 to 6.8 s at n = 28, hand-written C 2.3 s; queens-count 97 to 83 ms). Since C207h a loop checks its unchanged Float64s on its first step only (finite-checks.ts; queens-first 6.8 to 5.5 s, queens-count 90 to 72 ms, divisors 0.14 to 0.11 s). Since C207i a loop that only computes on Ints keeps all of them as int64_ts, a round leaving the small range starting again as written (collatz 0.29 to 0.21 s; since C207q its 3n + 1 is one test of n against the bounds, 0.21 to 0.18 s, hand-written C 0.154 s). Since C207j a Float64 made from a loop's count isn't checked finite (divisors 0.557 to 0.482 s at 20000 500). Left: divisors' product `floor(x / d) * d` is still checked, since it can overflow with x near the largest Float64 (dropping it would buy another 15%, given a bound on x); divisors' Float64 division; since C207l an array's or list's Float64 items known finite and not below zero aren't checked as read, nor the distance between two (queens-first 6.2 to 4.3 s at n = 28, hand-written C 2.3 s); since C207m `safe`'s fold gives false once it is (queens-first 4.3 to 3.4 s); since C207n those two hold under a search on the threads too (queens-count 0.46 to 0.40 s at n = 12 on one thread, hand-written C 0.12 s); since C207o `safe` enters its counted loop at once (queens-count 0.39 to 0.35 s, queens-first 3.4 to 3.1 s); since C207p a choice from a range counts through it with no list (queens-count 0.34 to 0.28 s, queens-first 3.2 to 2.5 s against hand-written C's 2.3 s); since C207r an array is lent to a function or loop that only reads it, no count taken and given back (queens-count 0.28 to 0.225 s, queens-first 2.5 to 2.3 s, level with hand-written C); since C207y a Float64 at most 2^1000 (a count, a range's item, one plus a number up to 2^940) is known, so the sum of two such needs no check, and with none left C207m's loop stops once false (langford at n = 13 natively on one thread 66 to 50 s, hand-written C 32 s; compiled at 10, 0.38 to 0.26 s); next queens-count's placed list (an array pushed onto per placement, hand-written C 0.12 s) Closed 2026-11-12 (Compiler lane), its leftovers measured each under 10% natively on one thread: queens-count at n = 12 0.155 s against a hand-written C count of 0.084 s on this machine; of its instructions (callgrind, n = 10) `safe`'s loop is 55%, its Float64 compares' NaN tests 6% of the time (bounded by building with -ffinite-math-only), the placed list's views (pushFront, new, dead, fields) about 12% of the instructions, and reusing the buffer's free slots when a view holds it alone measured no gain. collatz's counted loop is ten instructions a step with its range checks hoisted; the rest of its gap to a hand-written loop is the program calling `steps to one` for each fold. divisors' product check (15%) stays: dropping it needs a bound on x. | C207a-y (below), #2471 |

Under the 10% bar, so neither change kept: what would pay is the animals in shared memory (columns in a SharedArrayBuffer), not copies. So the pedal's arrays need backward modes for the array's jobs first (a push onto the front run backwards is a first and a rest), or `bands` kept a cons list as its own group (C93's per-value choice); (s3e1, s3e2) done (2026-11-05): `front of array` (std.ts) gives an array's first item and rest in one call, with a backward mode (the array made from them) in modes.ts, relate.ts and the written plans (compiler/src/modes.ts), and `array match` is written on it; `push front` and `empty array` run backwards too, and a definition that only calls a builtin on its inputs (`array cons`, `array nil`) is taken as that builtin, so a definition over arrays run from its answer ends with one answer at most (array-list.test.ts); (s3e3) measured with the pedal forced to arrays and s3a to s3d patched in by hand: it checks and plays on the core IR, the same samples bit for bit, but 1.7× slower per block than its cons lists (about 3.5 s against 2 s for a minute of sound) and 4 s slower to start, since its lists are short and built and taken apart item by item, which a cons cell does cheapest; so the tie-break question is answered without a policy change: the chooser's tie (the definition as written) is the right pick for the pedal, and what compile.ts's arrays buy it is P4a's writing the block's array in place, not arrays as such; the gain still can't be measured, and the tie-break question waits on it; (s4) done (2026-11-05, compiler/gaps/bench-audio.ts, which already runs both): the pedal at 0.050 ms a block on the core IR against compile.ts's 0.032 (1.57×), the hand-written pedal 0.018; (s5) done (2026-11-05, every example lowered with its own definitions as the editor does): no example reaches a compile.ts fallback. | C170 | No names or id strings in compiled code, closed. C170d: a `lab compile` module tags the program's own cases (and the core IR's boxes) by small numbers, with the map's `tags` saying which variant each number is. A stop carries its definition's name but no node or definition id; the map has those by the stop's number. So the module carries no node ids. Two cases keep the ids: a module whose solves the evaluator's solver runs, or with a hosts/js.js, since those make the program's values by id. The library's cases keep theirs for the runtime. Builtins are still bound by their library ids (`R.rt("text.split", …)`), the runtime's linkage, as a C program names printf. A page's script keeps its ids for the editor (C143 left). Every example's compiled output is the same as before. Also: core-map.test's C143c test compares functions lowered from the program, since calc's written reader gets fresh ids each lowering. | C94 | GAPS round 9, M4, the rest: in C, a record or data input that a function only reads fields of, or lends on in turn, is lent, not given. The caller passes it without a dup, and the function never drops it (Perceus's borrowed parameters, Reinking, Xie, de Moura and Leijen, PLDI 2021). Such a function is written as `name_b`, and calls in the program use it. `name`, the one the host and closures call, lends its inputs to it and drops them after. A function that makes a new value of the input's type is an update (machine's `stored`) and keeps its input, so the fields it moves on have one holder and are changed in place. A function that loops keeps its inputs too. Machine, `lab native`, median of 3: fib 20 146 → 103 ms, collatz 3000 562 → 480, sort 400 195 → 179, primes 5000 45 → 42. So `sum`'s body was at Int (its `0`), and a use at Float64 was sent to it uncopied. The default is now read through the body's own variables. | C108 | `read number` (L176) in C and WebAssembly. Both check text.ts's grammar (a sign, digits with perhaps a point, perhaps an exponent). C then calls strtod, which rounds once. WebAssembly reads the digits as an exact magnitude d and a power of ten e: for e ≥ 0 it rounds d·10^e once (mag.toNumber, ties to even); for e < 0 it takes d·2^s over 10^-e with at least 65 bits and the remainder as a last bit, then scales; at or below the smallest normal it takes the multiple of 2^-1074 nearest instead, so subnormals are rounded once too. Past the largest Float64 there is no answer, and -0 reads as 0. Also fixed: a module whose only big-number code is a runtime function on magnitudes didn't declare their array type. Tests: fixtures.ts `numberTexts` (the grammar's edges, halfway cases, the largest, subnormals, 1e±100000) in the WebAssembly "one answer or none" test and in C with every cell freed; 120,000 random decimals checked against the evaluator in WebAssembly by hand. Prior art: Clinger, "How to read floating point numbers accurately" (PLDI 1990), the exact big-number reading | #1165 |

| C106 step 1 | Random under `seeded` in WebAssembly and C (GAPS round 10 K4): ters/src/lang/random.ts's generator, xoshiro128** seeded by SplitMix32, in the runtimes (wasm-rt.ts `rand.*` on four i32 globals, c-rt.ts `lab_rand_*`), so every target draws the evaluator's numbers. `seeded(seed, body)` keeps the generator's words as it found them, seeds it, runs the body and puts them back, so an outer seeded goes on where it was; `random()` is the next number. A function whose row has Random is lowered (it runs under some seeded); a root that draws with nobody seeding it is refused, by name (the world's Random isn't there yet). An abort out of a seeded skips putting the words back: an outer seeded then draws from the inner one's generator (noted, not fixed). Test: fixtures.ts `randoms` (three draws at seeds up to 2³²+1, a seeded inside another, a helper that draws called from map's function), the evaluator's numbers in WebAssembly and in C with every cell freed. Prior art: Blackman and Vigna's xoshiro128** (2018, public domain); Steele, Lea and Flood's SplitMix (OOPSLA 2014), already credited in random.ts | #1143 |

It was refused, so any program that showed a list of texts or a record holding one didn't compile there. Found by the generated programs (C101b), which now show their Text answers too, with quotes, backslashes, tabs and controls among their texts. Test: fixtures.ts `shows` has `texts` (an empty text, quotes and a backslash, the lettered controls, NUL to 0x1f and DEL, é and an emoji, in a list), the evaluator's text in WebAssembly and in C with every cell freed | #1136 |

They found three more bugs, fixed here: (1) C leaked a cell when a match on a cell nobody else holds kept it for a new cell of the same fields, a way out of the arm freed it on the first try, and the second try (without it) still kept it (c.ts `takeApart`: what the first try did with it is undone); (2) WebAssembly trapped on an Int past an i64 given as a text position or code (`slice(t, 0, 2⁶⁴)`): such an Int is now the nearest end (wasm-rt.ts `int.clamped`), as text.ts clamps; (3) WebAssembly and C refused equal on values of a type nothing constrains (the items of a fold of `[]`): held as Float64, as C101 did for their lists. A Text result is given as is: `show` of a Text, quoted, isn't in WebAssembly and C yet (C102). 60 seeds of 60 definitions agree | #1133 |
| C101 | A generated differential test (REVIEW-2026-11-02c §3, asked for by the coordinator): small random typed definitions over Float64, Int, Bool and List Int (arithmetic, comparisons, if, let, a match on a list, map with a closure, the prelude's list jobs, `/`, `sqrt`, `log`, `mod`, `quotient` and `remainder` under `first`, numbers up to 1e300 and 2⁶²), each shown as text, run on the evaluator, compiled JavaScript, WebAssembly and C (compiler/test/fuzz.test.ts, fuzz-programs.ts). The evaluator's text is the meaning; an overflow the evaluator stops on may go unreported where nobody uses it (FLOAT-VALUES §2). 40 definitions in one program, so each engine builds once: about 3 s. `SEED=n` runs another set, `FUZZ=n` makes n. It found five bugs, fixed here: (1) compiled JS wrote an unused `let` of a record as `{ … };`, a block, a syntax error (compile.ts `bindLet`); (2) WebAssembly and C refused `[]` whose items nothing constrains, and map over it (scalar.ts: such a part is held as Float64, as no value of it is made; a root's own type variables stay refused, since its callers give values of them); (3) an Int literal past an i64 (1e300 as an Int) made WebAssembly fail to load (wasm.ts: a `$huge` of its limbs); (4) C wrote labels nothing jumps to, an error under -Werror (c.ts); (5) WebAssembly's `quotient` and `remainder` of an Int past an i64 by 0 trapped instead of having no answer (wasm-rt.ts). The C harness moved from c.test.ts to c-harness.ts to be shared. 60 seeds of 80 definitions agree. Prior art: Csmith (Yang, Chen, Eide and Regehr, PLDI 2011); QuickCheck's generators (Claessen and Hughes, 2000) | #1122 |

Test: fixtures.ts `caseNames` (the program's own cases, Optional's, a list's, a Bool, a record, a tuple, a number, a text), the evaluator's names in WebAssembly (wasm.test.ts) and in C with every cell freed (c.test.ts) | #1116 |
| C100 | `changed(value, path, part)` (L163) in WebAssembly and C, where it was refused as a cell operation though it is pure. With `data`, a path written out where it's used (`path` of a chain of fields, `item path`, `then path`) is read when compiling, and the value is rebuilt along it: a field step makes the record again with that field changed, an item step a function of its own that walks the list to the item (scalar.ts `changed`). A place that isn't there leaves the value as it was, as cells.ts changeAt does; a path computed at run time is refused with why. PLDI 2021). On top of C93's trees it bought no reliable gain: best of 7 runs, three processes each, collatz 3000 282-302 → 286-374 ms, primes 20000 199-246 → 186-197 ms, sort 1000 878-948 → 773-1020 ms, inside the noise; V8 makes a short-lived 8-field array cheaply. | C93 step 1 | GAPS round 9, M1: machine's registers, memory and code are lists read and written by position, and the chooser gave List up program-wide for three reasons machine has. A list of lists is no longer one (a list's rows never look at its items; only a list in a map's keys or a set's items counts). With Map and Set chosen first, lists are chosen again on what that made, those rows asked for as the program's own, so a Map or Set stored another way is no library holder, and its meaning stays as written. The boundary's library copies no longer change the originals. main's list inputs are stored on the way in. `map` is a job of the tree and array rows (linear; the tree's is log n deep where a map by match went n deep). Compiled primes 20000 47.9 → 0.47 s, `lab native` sort 1000 30.4 → 2.2 s and primes 20000 36 → 0.47 s, all agreeing with the ideal (compiler/test/machine.test.ts); every example agrees three ways (examples-agree, SWEEP=all); chapters now takes arrays and checks | #1093 |

| C94 step 1 | GAPS round 9, M4: in C, machine's `stepped` read `m.pc` after `executed(…, m)`, so the machine was dup'd for the call and every list in it had two holders while an instruction ran; `stored` changed `m.memory` while still holding `m`. Fields still needed are now read before the record is given to a call, and at the end of a function a record read field by field while one field is changed has every field read first (after the calls that take it whole), so the changed list has one holder. Perceus's borrowing (Reinking, Xie, de Moura and Leijen, PLDI 2021) is the prior art. primes 20000 native 61 → 36 s, every program's output the same. Test: compiler/test/c.test.ts "a record's fields are read before it is given away" | #1083 |

A fold over data that doesn't move, whose cases don't, is a constant (`clifford reverse`'s list of ones); a definition that is a record is its body's shape; a product of a product is no recursion. Such a match is now a generator (runtime.js `$handleG`) on the stack while its computation runs, passing the waits outward, and where code may wait an operation of such an effect is `yield* $performG`, which runs a waiting case as a generator outside its own handler, as `$perform` does. A value is used once by whatever changes it; reads (`value at`, `has key`, a field) borrow and must come first; a helper's parameters get a mode (read, taken, shared) found to a fixpoint; closures may only borrow, and one that is kept may capture none; a map of maps and a definition with Choice are refused. routes, ledger, chapters and wc pass; keeping an old version, giving a map twice, or keeping it in a closure fail. Prior art: Wadler's linear types (1990), Rust's borrowing, Perceus and Lean 4's reuse. Nothing chooses with it yet (C63b). Tests: ters/test/one-holder.test.ts | #843 |
| C30d2 | std Array's two ends in WebAssembly and C: `first of array` (no answer when empty), `rest of array` and `push front` on the same two-ended buffer; a push front onto the newest array writes into the free slot before it, onto a shared one copies, and in C a view nobody else holds moves on in place. A million pushes at the front run in linear time on both. Shaders refuse them for now (a shader's array has one size where it's made) | #754 |
| L125c1 | A Float64 is finite on compiled JavaScript, as in the evaluator (FLOAT-VALUES-2026-11-01.md §2): no answer is checked on the argument (`$div`, `$sqrt`, `$log`, `$pow`, `$mod`; none for a literal that can't be one), and an overflow late, with `$fin`, where it could vanish or decide something: a comparison, `equal`, `show`, `compare`, a divisor, `exp`, `atan2`, `range`, `whole part`. The stops are Aborts performed where the operation was, with the evaluator's reasons, so a handler of Abort in the program gets them; an overflow nothing uses isn't reported (Haskell's imprecise exceptions). `atan2` takes −0 as 0, as the evaluator does. Tests: compiler/test/finite.test.ts | #850 |
| L125c2 | A Float64 is finite on WebAssembly and C too: the lowering (scalar.ts) checks each Float64 operation the program calls as compiled JavaScript does (no answer on the argument, overflow late at a comparison, a divisor, `exp`, `atan2`, `show`, `range`, `whole part`), with a `stop` op whose reasons (compiler/src/stops.ts) are the evaluator's. WebAssembly's host throws the Abort, WASI writes the reason and exits with 1 (`wasi.stop`), C's `lab_stop` does the same, and C's test harness takes a stop as that call's answer. Unlike JS, a handler in the program doesn't get it; shaders have no checks. `atan2` takes −0 as 0 there too. The 9 WASM and C tests that fed ∞ or NaN since #842 now use finite inputs and compare the stops. Tests: compiler/test/finite.test.ts (WebAssembly, WASI), c.test.ts, wasm.test.ts | #867 |

The chooser weighs it only where the program runs on a host Map (the evaluator and compiled JavaScript: `lab run`, `compile`, `build-web`, `build-app`; never `lab native`), every map has one holder (C63a's check) and every key is an Int or a Text; a representation says so with the new `in place()` job. The empty map is one shared constant, so a put on it copies. A counting program (200,000 puts and lookups) runs about 8× faster compiled. routes still gets the tree, since the chooser counts each job once and routes lists its keys (C63c). Tests: compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts | #870 |

A lookup per town costs n log n on a tree and n on a table, and outweighs a listing of the keys done once. So routes takes Map's hash table on JavaScript, printing the same: 319 ms against 654 ms on the trees at 3000 towns (8.8× the ideal instead of 18×). bench-routes.ts has a `compiled, table` column. Tests: compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts, routes.test.ts | #874 |

No function is partial there. So the editor's CPU-side pictures can lower with `shader` and still draw in WebAssembly; a shader's arrays are refused there with the reason, and JavaScript draws them. Tests: compiler/test/int32.test.ts (WebAssembly against js-scalar.ts) | #878 |

So `first(() => a / b, () => -1)` is -1 at b = 0, and a handler of Abort no longer sees x / 0. An overflow still stops. WebAssembly and C already gave none in a partial function (#869); plain scalar code, which has no Choice, still stops with the reason. Tests: compiler/test/finite.test.ts | #879 |

Parts are only taken where the value is the answer, so a pair never feeds anything else. Fixed's unpolar now runs CORDIC's vectoring once for both length and angle. Left: quotient, remainder and power past 2⁶³ still trap (W5c) | #304 |
| C24 | WebAssembly tail recursion modulo cons: a function whose answer ends as cons(x, itself(…)) keeps the items so far in one more input and turns them round onto the answer at its other ends, so each step is a tail call (reversing, not filling holes, as WasmGC cells are immutable). ledger's entries: 15,000 lines ran out of stack, 200,000 now take 1.1 s | #303 |
| C23 | WebAssembly folds over `characters(t)` with no list and no stack: one function walks t's bytes from the end, a character per tail call (fusion, Wadler credited); the host fills a text four bytes a call. wc on 100,000 lines 4.2 s → 0.38 s (11×) | #302 |
| C7 W5a | Int in WebAssembly is an `i31ref` while it fits in 31 bits, else a `$big` box (now an i64, limbs in W5b), as Hoot and wasm_of_ocaml keep small ints (credited); the runtime's Int functions stay on i64s behind generated wrappers, the host makes and reads Ints with `Int.of` and `Int.get`. No measurable cost on ledger and wc | #301 |
| C7 W3d | A Number's show needs no host: past the plain decimals it is JavaScript's String, with Ryū's shortest digits written in WebAssembly (adapted, credited; tables built with BigInt into one global) and ECMA-262's layout. The same as String on every binary exponent, the decimal powers and random bits (2.6 million checked once, 30,000 in the test); the `host.show` import is gone, so modules import only Math and the world | #299 |
| C7 W3b-2 | Programs as WASI commands, with no JavaScript: the world written in WebAssembly over `wasi_snapshot_preview1`, and `_start` running main (`npm run wasi -- wc` in compiler/). wc, ledger, chapters, calc and a program using the rest of the world give the evaluator's output, files and exit codes under `node:wasi` on 90 runs, and run under wasmtime | #289 |
| C7 W4d | Searches with several answers in WebAssembly: a function that chooses takes the rest of the search as a closure and calls it on each answer (Mercury's success continuations, as the JS `$k` copies); handlers keep answers in a mutable box. queens and Countdown run whole; Countdown at 1.0-1.1× the hand-written ideal JS (compiled JS 1.2-1.5×) | #293 |
| C7 W4b | Choosing in WebAssembly: in first, optional, all and count, lets that choose `one of` a list (keep's shape, and nested) are a loop per choice, a tail call per item in the evaluator's order (all collects backwards and turns the list round); functions with Choice as values give a status too, and a generic definition is lowered per effect row as well as per type (keep with a test that may have no answer is a search itself). Every pure function of chapters and calc at known types compiles; 207 of their calls and the new searches fixture match the evaluator, a keep over 2×10⁴ items in constant stack | #280 |
| C7 W4a | Searches with one answer or none in WebAssembly: a partial function (Choice, or spending from a budget) gives a status after its answer (Mercury's semidet calling convention, credited); a call checks it and branches to the nearest handler's block, a tail call to a function that gives the same stays `return_call`; `first`, `optional`, `all` and `count` are blocks; `fail`, `value of`, and the Choice builtins (quotient, remainder, power, whole part, read, find, character code, character) are runtime functions with a status. Running out of a budget is no answer where it happens, which the nearest handler sees, as in the evaluator (the shader profile keeps its count check). chapters' `heading` and `lower` and ledger's `newline` and `table` now compile; 515 inputs match the evaluator | #279 |
| C7 W3a | Int and Text in WebAssembly: Int an i64 whose overflow traps (a runtime error, big Ints are W5), Text a UTF-8 byte array with positions counted in characters; compiler/src/wasm-rt.ts is a small runtime written in WebAssembly's text format (concat, length, slice, split, join all, characters, comes before, equal, show of Int and Number, range) that an assembler there turns into bytes, so the module's text is what runs. Library definitions (map, keep) now lower at known types (their body's type variables differ from their type's). wc's, chapters' and ledger's text functions match the evaluator | #276 |
| C7 W2 | Functions as values in WebAssembly: a function type is a closure struct holding its code (`(ref $code)`) and a code type taking the closure and the inputs, one rec group; a lambda is a function of its own plus a closure struct of what it captured; a definition by name is a closure made once; a call of a function value is `call_ref`, `return_call_ref` in tail position; a constant is a function of no inputs. Queens' `safe` (a fold into a function), numbers' `factorial` (a constant), calculus `newton` (takes functions) compile; answers match the evaluator | #273 |
| C7 W1c | Structural `equal` on data in WebAssembly (the evaluator's rule on each number, the last field a tail call: two lists of a million compare in constant stack); a fold whose arms each fold one child (lists, chains) is a loop, `down` keeping a stack of the values passed and `up` giving each its arm, the evaluator's order: polynomial over 10⁴ coefficients overflowed the stack, now 10⁶ run (375 ms) | #271 |
| C7 W1b | Data in WebAssembly (`data`): types with several cases and types that contain themselves (lists, expression trees) as a base struct with the case's number and a subtype per case, one recursion group; a match is `br_table`; a fold is a recursive function of its own, a result used once computed where it's used (insert stops at its place); a module that may not finish runs as in JS. sort, insert, polynomial, ∂polynomial/∂x, Countdown's valid and combine, algebra's derivative give the evaluator's answers through exported makers and readers (`List.cons`, `List.case`, `List.cons.head`) | #269 |
| C7 W1a | Records in WebAssembly as WasmGC structs, an exported reader per field (`Colour.red`), a generic definition at two types exported under both; the ray marcher's colours match the evaluator exactly. The editor's CPU fallback could now use WebAssembly for colour records (Inbox) | #265 |
| C7 design | docs/design/targets/WASM.md: whole programs to WasmGC by growing the scalar profile towards the heap; representation table, text as UTF-8 arrays, Int past 64 bits from the host, the five stages | #262 |
| C6 | G13 again, the part left: a single-answer function whose answers are one record leaves their fields in `$got` (a `$fields` copy) when its caller goes on with the answer; Countdown's `join` builds no `found`, 1.47× → 1.30× the ideal | #259 |

## Language lane

|---|---|---|
| C61c′2 | A keep and the Int folds (add, max, min) written in a page's handler go to the page's workers as a map does (branches-rt.ts `mapAway` with its `how`, scalar.ts at the keep and fold sites). Under the handler's step budget (run.ts `counted`, `stepsLeft`, `setStepsLeft`) each run on a worker or here gets what is left and says what it spent. The job spends their sum in order, so one that runs out of steps does so where it would in turn, and a handler that doesn't finish in turn doesn't on workers either. Test: page-workers.test.ts, collatz's keep in a handler (9000 numbers kept the same, 300000 running out on both) | #2251 |
| C61c′3 | A map, keep or Int fold in a function a page's handler calls goes to the page's workers too: called where it may wait, a function that has one (in itself or in what it calls, scalar.ts `reachesShared`) gets a copy of its own lowered as if it may wait (`func`'s `away`). Test: page-workers.test.ts, collatz's `steps of all` and `most steps` called from a handler (1000 numbers, the same sums, the jobs on workers); the page tests' clock now runs fast so a short first map starts the workers every time | #2253 |
| C61c′4 | A count or an all of a search in a page's handler (or a function it calls) goes to the page's workers, split by its first two choices as on Node (branches-rt.ts `searchAway`, the web's job code shared with `mapAway` as `awayRuns`). Where the pieces' steps pass the handler's, the search runs in turn to say. queens-count in a handler, in Chromium: 11 queens 715 to 313 ms on 4 cores. Test: page-workers.test.ts, queens 6 and 8 counted and gathered, the same on 3 workers | #2256 |
| C61c′5 | A first of a search in a page's handler goes to the page's workers too: the answer is the first piece's in order that has one (branches-rt.ts `searchAway`, `awayRuns`' `decided`). The pieces after it spend no steps and can't abort, as in turn. Test: page-workers.test.ts, queens 6 and 8's first board, the same on 3 workers | #2260 |
| C61e1 | Divide and conquer on Node: g(f(a), f(b)) in f's own body, f only computing, runs the two calls on worker threads with nothing written (pure-branches.ts `parallelHalves`, scalar.ts `both.on`/`both.pure`, branches-rt.ts `bothPure`). The top levels are unfolded by probes, the calls below shared out, and the levels above run again with their answers. Nothing that sets a budget: no per-call spend (scalar.ts `setsFuel` no longer counts reading the budget). examples/fibonacci: fib 38 2.3 to 0.9 s on 4 cores. Test: branches.test.ts | #2262 |
| C61e2 | A fold over a tree works out its two halves on workers on Node, nothing written: a fold whose arms only compute and whose two-answer arm needs both surely (pure-branches.ts `parallelTreeFolds`) gives each half to the fold's own function through `both.pure`. Tree of 64 leaves: 1.70 s in turn, 1.24 s on three workers. Test: branches.test.ts | #2264 |
| C61e3 | Divide and conquer on C's threads: a function's two calls of itself and a fold over a tree's two halves (scalar.ts `bothOnThreads`, c.ts `dcC`/`bothC`). The second call is pushed for any thread and the first made here; then the second is joined, or made here if no thread took it. Eight levels deep from the top; a failure anywhere reruns the top in turn, so aborts come in order. fib 38 natively 0.41 to 0.15 s on 4 cores. Tests: c-threads.test.ts | #2272 |
| C61e fix | Main red after #2264: scalar.ts `fails` asked the runtime about `both.on`/`both.second`, which only branches-rt.ts has, so 'no runtime function both.on' (core-names, fold-stack, branches). They now count as never failing. The tree test's leaves sum 600,000 products, since 60,000 now finish before the workers start. | #2277 |
| C61d′ | A page handler's map or keep on the GPU: its function printed as a WebGPU kernel where the shader lowering takes it (gpu-maps.ts; scalar.ts `stepped` lowers a root as under a budget; and, or, xor, shifted down as i32). The kernel counts the page's steps, each item given back to the CPU if it ran out, passed 32 bits or had no answer. Tried once past 5 ms and 1,000 items, kept while quicker. collatz below 300000: the same answer and 35,969,673 steps as the CPU. Test: gpu-maps.test.ts (headless Chromium, SwiftShader) | #2292 |
| C61d′2 | A page's GPU map whose function closes over the handler's values: the Ints and Bools it captured (scalar.ts `awayMaps` caps, the closure's order) go to the kernel after the item, the function lifted to a definition taking them (gpu-maps.ts). Also: a run the GPU refused or never wrote is worked out on the CPU (each item's steps start at −1, a validation error caught), where it used to read zeros as answers; a kernel with no captures binds none. Test: gpu-maps.test.ts counts the GPU's answers read back | #2295 |
| C42e | A fold whose join is no sum shares its items' work: where the item arm is `v = e(item); b(v, rest's)`, e never reading the rest's and calling something that may run long, b never reading the item, the fold is made one over `map(items, e)`, which goes to Node's workers by the map's own measure, and b puts the answers together in turn, so b needn't be associative (compiler/src/fold-items.ts, after fuse-steps; a fold of a map fused there comes apart again). A join C42b-d takes (an Int added, the larger or smaller kept) stays theirs. Measured first: a fold of a map was fused (C207c) and ran in turn, 6.5 s compiled and 2.1 s unfused. Example examples/fingerprint, an order-sensitive hash of Collatz blocks: compiled 6.1 to 2.1 s below 3000, the same output; collatz unchanged. Left: C's threads share an array's map only, so native stays in turn | #2299 |

The library's keep tests from the first item, so the items are held last first and the keep fails at the first item that fails, as in order. Example collatz native (its keep alone): below 3000000, 3.2 to 3.4 s on one thread and 1.3 to 1.4 s on four; the default 0.28 to 0.38 s and 0.12 to 0.14 s. Test: c-threads.test.ts (the evaluator's array, and its first abort, with 3 workers and with none) | #2216 |
| C42c | A fold of a list to an Int keeping the larger (or smaller) of the rest's result and an Int worked out from the item by calls that only compute (`if r < d then d else r`, through a let) is shared out as C42b's sum is (scalar.ts `itemFold`); the runtime puts the runs' answers together by `how` (branches-rt.ts `foldPure`, rt `fold.pure`, taking the empty list's Int too, worked out first as in turn). Int comparing is exact, so in any order. Example collatz's `most steps`: with `steps of all`, its default 3.6 s in turn, 1.95 s with three workers, the same answers, and the evaluator's at 3000 | #2214 |
| C42b | A fold of a list to an Int whose item's arm adds to the rest's result an Int worked out from the item by calls that only compute (pure-branches.ts `parallelFolds`) shares the items out on worker threads where they are worth it (map.worth): each run adds its items' Ints, then the runs' sums are added (branches-rt.ts `sumPure`, scalar.ts `itemSum`); Int addition is exact, so in any order. The items go from the last, as the fold in turn meets them, so an abort is the one it meets first. `array of List` folds whose arms don't read the rest as the items' own fold, read in place with no rest built (array-list.ts `foldOfItems`). Example collatz's `steps of all`: the whole program at its default 2.6 to 2.9 s in turn, 1.6 s with three workers, the same sum | #2210 |
| C43f | A `first` of a search that only computes and chooses (no Diverge) is shared by its first choice's items as a count is (C43d): item 0 runs on this thread first, and if it has no answer the others are shared out with every thread taking from the front, stopping past the first item with an answer, whose first answer is the result (branches-rt.ts `searchPure` with `how`, `sharedUntil`; `first.search`). A count, all or first of a search with one answer or none stays compiled as before (scalar.ts checks `several`), which also fixes a count or all of such a search giving 0 or []. Example queens' first solution agrees on workers and in turn; no speed claimed there, since its first item has an answer | #2202 |
| C43d | A `count` of a search written in place that only computes and chooses (pure-branches.ts `parallelCounts`) is lowered as the search, a closure, given to the runtime (scalar.ts, `count.pure`). The runtime runs it once for each item of the first choice a run makes (branches-rt.ts `countPure`, `choose.split`): the first run on this thread finds how many there are, and the others are shared out to workers. A search inside it keeps its own choices whole (`split.hold`, `split.give`). The counts add up to the whole count. A let in a shared function no longer keeps it from being shared (`ownEffects`). Example queens-count: 12 queens, 6.2 to 7.5 s in turn and 3.4 to 3.7 s with three workers | #2195 |
| C43e | An `all` of a search that only computes and chooses is shared by its first choice's items as a count is (C43d): each run gathers its answers, and they are joined in order into the list kind the type says, an array or a list of the runtime's (`all.search`). A search inside one being split keeps its first choice's split for the outer one (branches-rt.ts `answers` holds it). Example queens' all solutions agree; by hand, all boards of 12 queens 6.4 s in turn and 3.6 to 3.8 s with three workers | #2197 |
| C43a | A `keep` whose test, written in place, only computes shares its items on Node's worker threads like C42a's map (pure-branches.ts `parallelLists`, branches-rt.ts `mapPure` keeping each run's passing items). Example collatz: the numbers whose walk to 1 is long, 0.8 s to 0.5 s at its default, 2.9 s to 1.5 s below a million, the same numbers in the same order | #2169 |
| C42a | A `map` whose function, written in place, only computes (pure-branches.ts `parallelMaps`: List's map or the array's, not one that may both abort and run long) shares its items on Node's worker threads in up to 32 runs (branches-rt.ts `mapPure`, the same claims as C41's togethers); the compiled code keeps its own map and asks `map.worth` first, which says yes only when the call site's last measure makes the items a millisecond or more, so the small maps a loop makes many of cost what they did (a 3-item map 2 million times: 0.50 to 0.55 s, noise). | L43f2 | A record per mask in compiled JS (zero-parts.ts, after L43f1): a multivector holds only the parts its mask has. A record's or data case's field, and a closure's capture, take the mask of everything put there in the whole program (a list of bodies is never copied); a copy's inputs and answer, a function type's (from all its closures' codes), and a let's what it is set to; where two meet the value is copied into the other's record, with 0s or leaving out parts known to be 0. What the runtime or the world sees or makes, a root's inputs and answer, and what a page's handlers take keep every part; a cell holding only what it's given (cell, write, read) doesn't count as seen. NO_NARROW=1 turns it off. Example: Bounce's best of eight runs of 3000 solid steps is about 33 ms, against 41 ms with every part stored (65 ms before L43f1); zero-parts.test's steps answer as with every part | #2026 |

| C30 (history) | Moved from BACKLOG when the row was trimmed (2026-11-05): Lists as arrays. Done so far: P4a, #555, #558, #561 (pedal 2.9×). The split, join and block-in-array stages pay only together (~25% more); do them as one stage when another program needs lists as arrays. The aim: an `array` representation written in the language (REPRESENTATIONS.md), so WASM, C and the editor get it too. C30d (2026-10-31): Ersagun's two-ended array list; JS runtime views (C30d1, #734), the ends and `array of List` (#737), the ends in WebAssembly and C (C30d2, #754), the evaluator's arrays on the same buffer (C30d3a, #762; Ersagun chose "everywhere" 2026-10-31), the preview's chooser picking `array of List` from use (C30d3b), and the compiled targets taking the same choice (C30d3c: WebAssembly and C lower `first of array if any`). Next: the split, join and block-in-array stages, when a program needs them Steps, about an hour each (2026-11-05): (s1) done, measured by hand (2026-11-05, 3,000 chapters): chapters' word count (`split` by space, keep the non-empty, count) written over the array `split` gives takes the core IR's run from 213 to 150 ms and 252 to 166 (about 30%), while the same count kept on the cons list with its filter as a plain loop gains only about 5%, so the gain is the list being an array; (s2) done another way: `lab compile chapters` already stores its lists as `array of List`, and that ran 4.8× its cons-list time on the core IR (1,143 against 239 ms; gaps/chapters.ts measures the program as written, not as `lab compile` stores it), because the array's fold made a cons list of its items on every call; it now walks the array by position (scalar.ts arrayItems) and reads each item without a BigInt (core-rt.ts array.get), 1,143 to 710 ms; (s2b) done for the rest: on the core IR's JavaScript the rest an array's fold hands its arm is a view of the array from that item (scalar.ts restAsView, core-rt.ts array.from), chapters 710 to 545 ms; left (s2c): the 2.3× still against cons lists (239 ms) is the pair and the arm's closure call per item and the views made, and the chooser counting both folds as linear, so it picks arrays for chapters where cons lists are faster. Measured by hand (2026-11-05): the views are about a fifth of it (chapters stored, 525 to 405 ms with `array.from` made to give nothing, the same output), since no arm of chapters' folds reads its rest; but the arm is a closure the library's fold helper is given (core IR f19 to f20), so whether it reads the rest is known only where the helper is called. So (s2c1) done: a definition given a function written in place gets a copy specialized to it, its call direct, and a loop's record field handed only to an input nothing reads carried as it was (compiler/src/closure-args.ts, core IR's JavaScript): chapters stored as arrays 507-541 to 326-349 ms, against 239 on cons lists; (s2c2) started: `split` gives its pieces as a list read from the array it made (std.ts, ItemsList), so `array of` copies them once instead of walking cons cells: chapters stored as arrays 307-366 to 262-282 ms (cons lists 215); done (2026-11-05, Ersagun's yes): the chooser's pick of arrays for chapters, which `length` made (1 on an array, n on a cons list, weighed once per line in `words`): a priced function of one list at most linear, given a list a call or a let has just made, costs no more steps than making it, so it isn't weighed by a loop it is in and isn't a job where every use is so (represent.ts freshUses); chapters keeps cons lists (about 210 ms, 347 as arrays) and no other example's choice changes; done (2026-11-05): gaps/chapters.ts measures the stored program too (its `stored` way, the core IR of what `lab compile` stores, checked in chapters.test.ts and timed in bench-chapters.ts: 3,000 chapters 326 ms stored against 246 as written, on a slow day); (s3) the same in WebAssembly and C; (s4) block-in-array, measured the same way first. | before #1918 |

Steps of about an hour (2026-11-05): (a) measured (L72a in DONE): descent (C110) can't read `json printed` yet, for three reasons, one step each: (b) `join all(map(xs, f), sep)`, a list read item by item between separators (json list, object and quoted text all print this way): (b1) done in descent.ts and first-sets.ts, so the evaluator reads such a printer backwards (L72b1); (b2) done: the same in reader.ts for a list of the printer's own values (`read <printer> list`), so compiled targets read it too; an item read by another function is left for (d); (c) show of a Float64 read back (JSON's numbers): done in descent.ts (L72c), the written reader too (c2, done: reader.ts `read float`, the longest text `read number` reads and show gives back exactly); (d) done (L72d): `json printed` reads back by descent in the evaluator, as `json read` reads compact JSON: a function of one character is read back from what it writes for the first 128 characters, worked out forward once, and itself elsewhere; a match on an input still to be read reads each case; an input passes into a definition read through its body; (d2) done: the same in reader.ts, so compiled targets read `json printed` backwards too (L72d2); then (e) done as a design note (GRAMMAR-DESCENT-2026-11-05.md "JSON: one relation"): `json read` accepts all of RFC 8259 and `json printed` writes one spelling, so JSON becomes `json spelled`, a Choice of every spelling with the canonical first; `json printed` is its first and `json read` it run backwards; (e1) done in part (L72e1): `blank`, `json character spelled` (itself, \\uXXXX in either case, the short escapes) and `json spelled` in the prelude, its first spelling json printed's text and run backwards by descent reading blanks and escapes as `json read` does (json-spelled.test.ts); (e1b) done: a number spelled any way `json read` reads it (`1.0`, `1e3`: `json number spelled`, show's first, then `inverse(json number read, x)`, L72e1b); left: (e1c) done: \\uXXXX for any character (`json string character`: json character spelled, or `inverse(json escape read, c)`), read by descent and the written reader, json read's speed or better on 111 KB (L72e1c); surrogate pairs done too (L72e1c2: `\\uD83C\\uDF89` joined, as json read joins them); (e1d) `json printed` defined as `json spelled`'s first (its hash moves); (e3) measured and fixed (L72e3): descent reads `json spelled` faster than `json read` reads, 0.55 s against 1.34 s for 111 KB of indented JSON, once a definition's spellings of a character are kept; (e2) done in descent.ts (L72e2): a piece written `one of(texts)`, or `if one of([…]) then a else b`, reads every alternative and keeps the one that reads farthest, the first of those that tie, so a blank reads any run greedily (descent-choice.test.ts); (e2b) done: the same in reader.ts, so compiled targets read `json spelled` too (L72e2b); (f) done (L72f): `json read` is `json spelled` run backwards, the hand-written reader deleted; left: (f3) json read's message says where the text stopped and what was expected, as calc's lines do (L70c), (e1d) `json printed` as `json spelled`'s first (L72e1d1: fast in the evaluator now, but the core IR runs all of json spelled inside its Choice and runs out of stack, so it waits for the core IR to run a `first` of a Choice without nesting), (e4) `json printed indented` as a variant of json spelled with its blanks first | before #1918 |

sorting marks after an `x` makes fi agree, and reading ǖ as ü and a macron makes sv nearly agree but breaks is and nb (ǻ), so both wait for a closer look; (e5b4) letters that sort as one (da aa, cs ch), which the build doesn't find;
