| E103 (history) | Moved from BACKLOG when the row was trimmed (2026-11-05): **Bug.** Adding a value in a definition run backwards (physics `fall`) takes about 4 s; five stages (#478 to #504) took fall's holes from 48 s to 18.5 s in all. Stopped by the 10% rule. **Measured again 2026-11-01:** 23 holes 121 s, the worst 22 s, of which the plan comparison per offer 5.7 s and whole checks for offers whose plan changed 10.4 s; E133a took the node copies off (→ 18 s); E133b took the analyzer's full scan per offer off (worst 12.7 s, all 23 holes 67 s); E103c (#898) checks once for every offer that makes the same of the group's types and plans, each offer's units on their own (fall's 23 holes 80 → 47 s, the worst 16 → 8 s, measured on a busy machine; same offers); E103d (#902): a filled program's solves and calls are listed from the program's, not by scanning its ~10,000 nodes in `useDirections` and the jobs check (all 23 holes 50 → 30 s, the worst 10.6 → 4.2 s; same offers); E103e (#938): an offer's units are checked once, not again inside its whole check (23 holes 30 → 23 s, the worst 4.2 → 3.7 s); **measured again 2026-11-01 21:40** (busy machine, all 23 holes ~31 s): the plans compared per candidate 8.2 s (7,452 plans, ~1.1 ms each), the type-group checks 10 s (2,229 distinct groups, ~4.5 ms each: an offer of another shape makes fall's own type another shape, so few share), the whole checks of risky offers 7.7 s (400, ~19 ms each; checking their units apart buys nothing). Stopped by the 10% rule: the next stage needs a new idea, such as checking a group's callers against its new type instead of a whole inference, or a plan that can tell from the filling alone that it can't change; what's left is the checks' own work. **Measured again 2026-11-02** (all 23 holes 29.7 s, the worst 5.2 s, fall's whole body): that hole asks 1,221 plan comparisons and 645 distinct type-and-plan checks for 113 offers kept, because a changed plan is part of the check's key and most candidates change it a little. **Measured again 2026-11-04** after E248 and E250 (L211e and the steady module had made it slower): fall's 17 holes 64 s, the whole body's hole 9 s after the first; what's left is the checks' own work (inference of the group, 7 of 9 s). **Measured again 2026-11-05:** fall's 23 holes 127 s, the worst 32 s: each check of a filling scanned every node of the program and the library for the uses that close an input's row (infer.ts closingUse, 11 s of the worst hole); L226 (#1647) scans only the group being checked (23 holes 127 → 72 s, the worst 32 → 18 s, the same offers). What's left: the plans compared per offer (solveMode, ~4 ms each, 2,019 for one hole with 7 distinct answers) and the checks of the group. A plan per builtin class (modes.ts builtinGoal gives most builtins one forward way) would save under 10%: 318 of a hole's 2,019 plans are builtins', 1,187 definitions'. The next idea: where only a plan changes, check the solves' verdicts instead of a whole inference. Hole 0 again (17.8 s): 948 call fillings each analyse fall's one solve afresh (7.5 s; the analyzer is per Code, and fall's own lambda is not kept as a published one is), for 5 distinct verdicts. Reusing a verdict across callees needs a fingerprint of the callee's modes in every known set and its size-change graphs, none cheap and sound yet | before #1918 |

And a letter with two accents whose first makes a letter (Swedish ǖ: ü, then a macron) is taken as one letter; read as ü and a macron, sv nearly agrees, but is and nb then differ on ǻ. Both kept out until looked at more closely (BACKLOG L214) | this PR |
| L214e5b3 | Collation tailorings for languages that put their own script first: root's primaries now count 1,024 apart (collation-tables.ts `TAILORED`), so a whole alphabet fits before Latin, and a tailoring's primary is saved in five characters. be, bg, kk, ky, mk, mn, ru, sr and uk give the host's order (about 490 code points each), and az now does too; each language is checked on its own script's letters. 28 languages in all; el, sv, fi and others stay root (BACKLOG L214e5b2c). cases.test.ts checks ru and uk as well | this PR |
| L214e5b2b | Collation tailorings for more languages: a locale's accents' secondary weights are put in root's as its letters' primaries are (each element against root's for the same code point, the longest run in root's order kept, the others just after it), so pl, hu, da, et, lt, ro, sk, tk and vi now give the host's order too; 18 languages in all (wo left, now differing). cases.test.ts checks pl and hu as well | this PR |
| L214e5b2 | Collation's locales in Ters, for targets with no host: the cases module's `table collated(a, b, locale)` uses the locale's language's tailoring over the root tables, the code points whose elements in that locale's order aren't root's (collation-tables.ts `tailoring`: each of the locale's primaries is the root primary its letters share where they keep root's order, and a letter of its own comes just after the last kept, root's primaries counting 64 apart). library/collation.ts builds them off the host's `Intl.Collator` for the Latin-script languages and keeps those whose tailored tables give the host's order on random words of their letters: af, cs, eo, es, fil, gl, sl, tr (72 code points), wo and yo; the rest stay root (BACKLOG L214e5b2b). Tests: cases.test.ts against the host in tr, es, cs and sl | this PR |
| L214e5b1 | Measured, not built: locales' collation tailorings for the targets with no host. collation-tables.ts's build, run on a locale's own `Intl.Collator` (7 to 9 s each), agrees with the host on 2,116 pairs of test words for es, de, hu, pl, tr and phonebook German; it disagrees on da (92, `aa` sorts as one letter), cs (22, `ch`) and sv (4, ø against ő at the second level), which it can't find. A tailoring against root is small when counted as the code points it moves (the ones outside the longest run in root order): tr 3, fr-CA 9, sv 12, de 0. A locale that puts its script first moves hundreds (ru 443, el 406, hi 719), better kept as a reordering of primaries, and ja's Han order moves 35,739. Steps e5b2 to e5b6 in BACKLOG L214 | this PR |
| L72e1d1 | Bug from L72f (#1881): `json read` compiled through the core IR ran out of stack on about 300 bytes of JSON. Its written reader tested each character by `json character spelled`, a Choice, and the core IR runs the rest of a Choice inside it, so each character nested the rest of the reading. reader.ts now takes that function's first spelling (`first`), and 580 KB reads in about 1 s. `json character spelled` also works out each spelling only when asked (nested `if one of`, read by descent.ts's `written`), so `first(json spelled(v))` prints 300 records in 0.29 s against 2.2 s in the evaluator. `json printed` as that first stays parked: on the core IR it runs out of stack the same way, since all of json spelled runs inside its Choice (json.test.ts: a 200-record text read compiled) | this PR |
| C110 s5d | calc.test's random sessions run compile.ts's way again, all four ways agreeing on 600 random lines: since L72f's inverses.ts change, compile.ts reads calc's lines through the written reader (20 lines in 17 ms, against about a second a line before). The C21 test stays on the core IR, since compile.ts's written reader recurses on JavaScript's stack | this PR |
| L72f | `json read` is `json spelled` run backwards: `first(inverse(j ↦ json spelled(j), json unblanked(t)), abort("json read: not JSON: …"))`, read by descent in the evaluator and by its written reader in the core IR's JavaScript. The hand-written reader (`json value` and the eight definitions under it, L190b) is deleted, so JSON is printed and read by one relation. Its message no longer says where the text stopped (json.test now expects "not JSON"). inverses.ts expands the library's inverse of a printer (only leaving its inverses of plain definitions to descent), so the core IR reads json read through the written reader. JSON's law (json read undoes json printed, indented too), json.test, json-by-type.test and json-spelled.test pass | #1881 |
| L72f1 | json.test and json-by-type.test check the evaluator against the core IR's JavaScript (lowerCore, loadCoreJs), as the editor and `lab build` run programs, in place of compile.ts, whose search would run json read written as json spelled run backwards out of memory (L72f). json-by-type's Map, Set and Bytes test stays on compile.ts: the core IR doesn't bring in the library's bytes of base64 for `json of` yet | #1878 |
| L72e1c2 | `json escape read` joins a surrogate pair, `\\uD83C\\uDF89`, into the one character it spells (RFC 8259 §7), as json read does, so `json spelled` read backwards by descent or the written reader takes any character json read takes (json-spelled.test.ts) | #1874 |
| L72e1c | `json spelled` reads `\\uXXXX` for any character, as json read does: a string's character is `json string character`, json character spelled or `inverse(json escape read, c)` (\\u and four hex digits, `hex number` and `from code`). Descent reads a Choice of ways to write a character, each way, the farther kept; an `inverse` whose function's guard tests its first character (`json escape start`) is tried only where that character passes, so a run of hex letters costs nothing (14 KB of hex hashes: 36 s before, 0.18 s). The written reader does the same. Measured against json read: 111 KB of records 0.85 s against 1.39, the hashes 0.18 against 0.11, 8.6 KB of \\u escapes 0.24 against 0.23. Also fixed: inverses.ts left the library's own `inverse(json number read, x)` to descent instead of solving it, which since #1860 had made withInverses give up on every program (calc on C and WebAssembly in descent.test) | #1871 |
| L72e3 | Measured: descent reading `json spelled` against the hand-written `json read`, on JSON.stringify(items, null, 1) of 10, 100 and 1,000 records (1 KB to 111 KB). At first descent took 6.5 times as long (7.9 s against 1.2 s at 111 KB), nearly all of it working out json character spelled forward for each plain character of a string. With a definition's spellings of a character kept (descent.ts `spellingsOf`), it takes 0.09, 0.2 and 0.55 s against json read's 0.04, 0.22 and 1.34 s, the same values each time. So `json read` as json spelled run backwards (L72f) is faster from about 10 KB | #1864 |
| C110 s5b2 | wasm.test runs calc after C110 s5: its whole programs (WebAssembly, the core IR's JS, WASI) and its Abort test write calc's reader as code first (inverses.ts `withInverses`), as compiled targets read it, and the Abort test reads each line with `whole expression` directly, since calc has no hand parser's tokens step now. Three tests failed on main after #1851 | #1860 |
| L72e1b | `json spelled` spells a number any way `json read` reads it: `json number spelled(x)` is show's spelling first, then `inverse(json number read, x)`, the texts of digits, signs, ".", e and E that read number reads as x. Descent and the written reader read `inverse(g, x)` for x as the longest run of the characters g's guard takes (`all pass(characters t, p)`) that g gives an answer for; descent works out a builtin with Choice (read number) forward as its first answer. So `[1.0, 1e3, -0.5E+2]` reads as json read reads it (json-spelled.test.ts) | #1860 |
| L72e2b | The written reader (reader.ts) reads a Choice piece as descent does, so compiled targets read `json spelled` too: `one of(texts)` the longest first; `if one of([…]) then a else b` each arm read and the one that reads farther kept; and a definition that calls itself (`blank`), given only known inputs, gets a reader of its own, `read <name>(text, at, …)`, giving where it ends. Run by the evaluator, it reads as descent does (descent-choice.test.ts, json-spelled.test.ts) | #1858 |
| L72e1 | JSON as one relation, its first part (L72e): the prelude's `blank` ("" first, or a space, tab, line end or carriage return and a blank), `json character spelled` (json character's spelling first, then \\uXXXX in lower and upper case, then the short escapes) and `json spelled`, every text that spells a Json value with blanks between the tokens. Its first spelling is json printed's text, and descent reads it backwards as `json read` reads blanks and escapes (test/json-spelled.test.ts). For it descent's character table takes every spelling of a function written `one of(…)`, through lets. Not yet: other number spellings, \\u escapes past 127 | #1856 |
| L72e2 | Descent reads a Choice piece (JSON as one relation, L72e): a printer may write several texts, the one it prints first, and descent reads `one of(texts)` and `if one of([…]) then a else b` by reading every alternative and keeping the one that reads farthest, the first of those that tie. So a `blank` ("" first, or a space, tab or line end and a blank) reads any run greedily, and the printer's own text still reads (test/descent-choice.test.ts). Descent's forward evaluation also builds a list literal | #1854 |
| C110 s5 | The last step of C110 (a printer read backwards by recursive descent): calc's run line reads the line with `read expression`, the printer run backwards, and the hand parser (expression, sum, product, unary, power, atom, Parsed) is deleted, 378 nodes. `whole expression(t)` is `first(read expression(inverse(spaced, t)), abort("not an expression: " + t))`; `%` reads at product strength. The readings change with it: an error is "not an expression: <line>", `2 ^ -1` has no reading (the printer writes `2 ^ (-1)`), and `007` has none either (descent.ts's int show now reads no leading zeros, as show writes none). calc.test's random sessions and C21 test run the core IR's way, since compile.ts's way takes about 1 s a line | #1851 |
| L72d2 | The written reader (reader.ts) reads `json printed` backwards as descent does, so compiled targets can too. It gained the same pieces as descent: a list of items read by another function of one input through its body (an object's field pairs); a match on an input still to be read; an input passing into a definition read through its body; and the characters of a Text read back through a function of one character's table, which descent.ts works out (`read characters by json character`). Run as a definition by the evaluator, it gives what descent gives (test/descent-lists.test.ts) | #1847 |
| L72e | Design note added to GRAMMAR-DESCENT-2026-11-05.md, "JSON: one relation". `json read` accepts all of RFC 8259, but `json printed` writes one spelling. So JSON's grammar becomes `json spelled`, a Choice of every spelling with the canonical one first (blanks between tokens, any number spelling, any escape). `json printed` is its first answer, and `json read` is it run backwards. Descent's one new rule is to read a Choice piece by its alternatives in order. Steps e1, e2 and f are in BACKLOG | this PR |
| L72d | The prelude's `json printed` reads back by descent, so the evaluator runs it backwards in one pass and gets what `json read` gets on compact JSON (escapes, nesting, empty lists and objects; test/descent-lists.test.ts). Three additions to descent.ts make this work. A function of one character (`json character`) is read back from what it writes for the first 128 characters, worked out forward once and longest first; any other character reads as itself where the function writes it as itself. A match on an input still to be read reads each case with its fields. An input still to be read passes into a definition read through its body (`json quoted(t)`). Descent's forward evaluation also gained `first`, a character's code, characters, `join text`, shifts and bit-and, and binding a case's fields | this PR |
| L72c | Descent reads a Float64's show backwards. It takes the longest spelling there whose number `show` writes back exactly, which is what relate.ts's search accepts. So `-2.5`, `0.3` and `1e-12` read, while `1.0`, `+1`, `0.30`, `.5` and `1e3` don't, since show never writes them. first-sets.ts gives such a piece a digit or "-" to start with. The evaluator now reads a printer of Float64s and lists of them backwards (test/descent-lists.test.ts) | this PR |
| L72b2 | The written reader (reader.ts) reads a list of the printer's own values between separators, as descent does: `read <printer> list(text, at, sep)` reads one item or more, and an empty list is read where there is none. Nested lists read the same as descent reads them, run as a definition by the evaluator, the way compiled targets run it (test/descent-lists.test.ts) | this PR |
| L72b1 | Descent reads a list between separators: `join all(map(xs, f), sep)`, with xs an input list (or the characters of an input Text), reads each item by f backwards, with sep between items, and accepts no items too. A trailing separator or a missing item gives no reading. first-sets.ts lets such a piece start with anything. A printer of nested lists, `[1,[2,3],[]]`, reads back in the evaluator (test/descent-lists.test.ts) | this PR |
| L72a | Measured, not built: can descent (C110) read `json printed` backwards, replacing the hand-written `json read`? Not yet. first-sets.ts refuses it at `json number`, a Float64's show. Reading on through its body, it also needs a list read between separators (`join all(map(xs, f), sep)`, used by lists, objects and quoted text), and `json character` read back through guards on the character's code, which descent can't solve. Each of these is now a step of L72, plus whitespace between tokens | this PR |
| L206a | Measured, not built: the evaluator staging known-data calls (stage.ts `stageProgram`, as the compilers do) in place of the table product jet. Staging the program takes 5.3 s, so it costs more than it saves for one run, and the staged run is still 2.8× the jet's. The jet stays until a library can declare it (L206b) | this PR |
| C110 s5a | Descent reads a name the way calc's hand parser did, a letter and then letters or digits (`v9`). | L229a | The steps of an effect, designed (ANSWERED-LATER.md "The steps of an effect"): `steps(E, c)` runs c to its next operation of E and gives it back as a derived `Steps E R ε`, one case per operation with its rest, and `done`; matched with the handler's own arms. | L44g | LU kept for many right-hand sides: `factored(m)` does solved's elimination once and keeps it as std's `LU factors` (LAPACK's getrf: the pivots in the order taken, each pivot's row of U, and the multiple of it taken off each row below, by that row's place), and `solved with(factors, b)` does to b what elimination did to the matrix's last column, then reads the triangle back as `solved` does (getrs). | L215d2 | Measured, not built, and (d3) with it: giving the palette's trials (infer.ts `fillings`) a layer so the check cache answers them. A hole's fillings take 2.5 s in physics and 2.8 s in constructions (about 200 offers a hole), each trial a recheck of the hole's group and its dependents through `reuse`, 72 to 103 ms. A whole check of the same program through a warm cache is 128 to 169 ms, slower, since a filling changes the group the trial is about and the cache's keys cost more than `reuse`'s reading of the rest. So `reuse` stays, as the trials' faster path, and (d3) is dropped. The palette's time is the number of trials, not each one's check | this PR |

Greek (el) joined the tailored locales: its capitals drop their accents, 228 code points. The same as the host's `uppercase`/`lowercase` in en, tr, az, lt and el on words and every cased code point below U+0600 (test/cases.test.ts), except what is left: Lithuanian's dot before an accent, Greek's combining accents and added diaeresis, case-ignorables between a letter and a final sigma. | L215d1 | signatures.ts `recheck` (and its `fill` and `callers`) removed: only modules.test called it, and the check cache does what it did, an edit that keeps a definition's type stopping there since a caller's key holds its callees' schemes (a pin's when pinned). Its test is now the cache's: an edit inside pinned `evaluate` checks one group again, a type-changing edit checks its callers, and both find what a whole check finds. The spread test went with it, check-cache.test's random edits compare `spread` already | this PR |
| L189e2 | The world's bytes cross as a copy: the list `read bytes`, a file's bytes and a Response's body give is made from the world's bytes as it is walked (bytes.ts `BytesList`, the same value as its cells written out, as an array's view is), so storing it as a byte array copies them (std.ts `array of`) and `write bytes` copies them out, with no list made between. A function that performs a hosted operation is partial (scalar.ts, in C too), so an unhandled one is the program's Abort, not a trap. A number edited in Constructions checks in about 100 ms with the cache (about 370 before, every group missed), Raymarch's in 85 (230). Test: test/check-cache.test.ts, the random edits on more examples, and an edit to a constant against one to an implementation | #1685 |

An operation the file doesn't answer is a refusal to build that names it; `lab hosts <program> --c` prints one to start from. `lab run`, `compile` and `native` let main leave the program's own effects to its host file (run.ts mainProblem). | L203g | An algebra found by its type, not by `clifford algebra`'s name (GRADES.md "Any library's algebra"): a definition with no inputs whose value is an `Algebra`, however it is made (a function of the program's own, the record written out), is read as one. Bounce, Spacetime and Constructions compile to the same JavaScript (test: grades.test.ts) | #1677 |
| L203e | The versor inverse read from its own code (GRADES.md "Any library's algebra", step 4): an arm or an otherwise that fails gives no value, so it adds nothing to a match's union, and ṽ / ⟨v ṽ⟩₀ has the reverse's blades. The prelude's operations still read by name are those whose rule is sharper than their code: the sandwich and the reflection (they keep grades), the projection, the exponential, and the parts by blade. Bounce, Spacetime and Constructions compile to the same JavaScript (test: grades.test.ts) | #1676 |
| L203d | The duals and the regressive product read from their own code too (GRADES.md "Any library's algebra", step 4): a multivector with no part on is known as the record of zeros, so `element of(blades, blade, zero)` with a known blade runs, and a known call's result is read as a mask. Masks of Bounce, Spacetime and Constructions take 214, 310 and 365 ms (243, 353 and 894 before L203c) and compile to the same JavaScript. Still by name: the versor inverse (its code checks a number), the sandwich, the reflection, the projection, the exponential and the parts by blade, whose rules are sharper than their code (test: grades.test.ts) | #1675 |
| L203c | The prelude's Clifford operations read from their own code where that gives the same masks (GRADES.md "Any library's algebra", step 4's first half): the geometric, outer and inner products, the reverse and the involution are no longer read by name. For the outer and inner products, a definition called with only known inputs (and functions written in place that read nothing around them) runs when its type says it is pure and it may stop, so `clifford table where(squares, basis, wanted)` is a known table. A call's inputs are now read once, and a library call given no function is kept by its inputs, so the masks of Bounce, Spacetime and Constructions take 176, 284 and 192 ms (243, 353 and 894 before), and they compile to the same JavaScript. Two algebras on one record (a program's own and a library copy's) now have masks that combine, as a mask is over the record's parts. Still by name: the duals, the regressive product and the versor inverse (their code looks up a blade's element, which the reading doesn't follow), the sandwich and the reflection (they keep grades), the projection, the exponential, and a part named by literal vectors (test: grades.test.ts) | #1671 |
| L203b | Grade masks read through known data (GRADES.md "Any library's algebra", steps 1 to 3): grades.ts knows a value before running (the algebra's own, a definition's value that runs when a match or a fold takes it apart), runs only the matching case of a match on one, folds known data case by case with the recursive results folded first, keeps only the parts both sides have in a `zip parts` with ·, none in a `map parts` by a weight known to be 0, and reads `total` as zero when no part is on. | L29 | A function input's rows checked in any order, third try (the first two are in #1602's row): before a function's body is checked, each input it gives to a case's field, or to a definition already checked, whose type there is a function with a closed row takes that type (infer.ts `closingUse`). A call of the input before that use then puts its row inside the call's, as a field's function's always did, instead of giving it the call's row. `f` called under `first` and then boxed in a field of type (Float64) → Float64 is pure either way; with nothing closing it, the call's row is f's as before. Nothing looks at types at run time: where each is used both types are known, and a code → code pass (src/lang/fields-by-type.ts, run with arith.ts's) gives the call a generated definition with one arm per case, so the evaluator, relate.ts and every target see an ordinary match. The checker asks for both types where it is used (once its inputs are written: with holes, the holes say so), so a definition generic in the value's type can't use them (a later step could copy such definitions per type, as arith.ts does for + − ·). One level deep: a `List Arg` field is kept. Prior art: GHC's `deriving Functor`, "Scrap your boilerplate" (Lämmel and Peyton Jones, TLDI 2003) gmapT. Plain data only, like show, and the names are the program's, as show's are (the compiled program carries them only when it uses one); run backwards, it runs forwards once its value is known, as show does. machine's `op name` is one call instead of 21 arms. ## Language lane B

The second Language lane, from the 10-hour run of 2026-11-05/06: the sharing chain (L109 to L115, L17) and the physics programs (L120 to L124).

| id | item | PR |
|---|---|---|
| I229 | MCP `step(at, …)` puts the let around the innermost body holding the part (a function's, a let's steps after it, a case's), not around the whole run of steps (mcp/session.ts stepBefore): at the hole after `a = …` it goes after `a`, so a sequence is built top-down, each step naming the ones before, and inside `all(() => □)` it goes inside, where `one of`'s Choice is handled (before, it went outside `all` and was refused). The "did you mean p, i?" for a one-letter name is the Editor lane's (editor/typed.ts closest). Test: ters/test/mcp.test.ts | this PR |
| I228 | MCP `replace` reads a function whose body is on the lines after its `=>` (an outsider's `all(() =>` with three steps under it): the text around it is read with the body a hole, then the body into that hole as text of its own (mcp/session.ts blockIn), so its steps bind, each in the next, and the reader never sees them. Closing brackets at the body's end that it doesn't open go back to the text around it. Three steps took 55 s and were refused; now about 1.4 s, one step 1 s. Test: ters/test/mcp.test.ts | #2550 |
| I227 | A function generic over a number type runs backwards from a number that isn't whole: `total price(net, rate) = net · (1 + rate)` is Int's by its whole literal, as forwards, and given rate 0.2 and 300 relate.ts now runs its copy at Float64 (arith.ts resolveArithmetic's `want`), as a caller at Float64 would, and answers net 250; given whole numbers it stays Int's. An Int operation given a number that isn't whole says so in Ters's words, naming the operation, where the host's BigInt error came out. BACKWARDS.md's table has the row. Tests: ters/test/backwards.test.ts | #2546 |
| L243 | agent is back in core-js.test's agreement tests (main, and the page before and after its handlers), with its two trips' goals nearer: (3, 3) and (7, 2), the program otherwise as it is. The evaluator takes 2.6 s on it there, against 6 minutes at the page's own trips (core-js-agent.test.ts still checks those in the nightly). Measured why: the coupled search keeps the cells it reached in a map and its states in a queue, both lists in the evaluator, so each step costs more as they grow (140 states looked at, 1 s; 810, 11 s; a whole trip about 3 minutes, a quarter of it in the library's put entry and entries have, a fifth collecting garbage) | #2538 |
| L236b | Android's permission dialog: a native page's handler that asks for a permission waits for the answer. `ask permission` is the world's on C (c-rt.ts world.askPermission; a command line says yes). A page that asks runs each handler on a fiber of its own (c.ts, c-rt.ts fibers, sigsetjmp on Android) that parks where it asks; the page shows what the handler wrote so far, and ters_page_permission, the screen's answer, goes on from there, the handled operations' frames it had put back. The screen is asked (ters_ui's new `ask`) from the page's stack, not the fiber's. MainActivity asks with checkSelfPermission and requestPermissions, onRequestPermissionsResult answers through JNI (nativePermission), and the manifest names camera, microphone, location and notifications when the page asks. An event while a handler waits runs at once and hears no unless already given. Tests: compiler/test/c-permission.test.ts (ucontext and Android's fibers), c-native-app.test.ts (JNI on the stand-in activity) | #2510 |

| L236 (step 3) | A page's handler may ask for a permission: Permissions is in the Handler row (std.ts PAGE_HANDLER), and the handler waits for the person's answer as for a fetch. The build also moved dice, routes and pond onto the prelude's copies of definitions they had written themselves (same hashes), which main had left unbuilt. | C201 | A `together` whose actions only compute (pure-branches.ts `parallelTogethers`) runs them on L101's threads in C, not one after another on fibers (scalar.ts `par:together`, c.ts `togetherC`): the actions, marked shared, are all shared out at once, as they are few and each may be long, unless the together took under a millisecond the last time; then they run on the calling thread from the first till they have taken one. An action that fails on another thread runs again in its turn, so the together fails at the first action that does, as in order. Example: examples/area native, side 2400, 2.5 s before and 0.86 s on four cores, the same answer; compiler/test/c-threads.test.ts (a big Int the actions share; actions 2 and 4 abort on threads, the together's abort is 2's), ThreadSanitizer clean | #2173 |
| L101 | Multithreading where uniqueness allows it: in C, a map of an array whose function only computes (pure-branches.ts `parallelMaps`, as compiled JavaScript shares on workers since C42a) runs its items on threads (scalar.ts `par:map` with `threads`, which `ters native` sets; c.ts `parC`). The items run from the last, as the library's map does, on the calling thread till they have taken a millisecond, then on a pool of threads kept from the first (the cores less one, or TERS_WORKERS; TERS_BRANCHES=say counts the items they ran). Counts are what makes threads unsafe in C: each thread has its own status, cells and free lists, and what the threads share (the function and the items, through lab_shares) is marked shared first, so only those cells' counts change atomically (Lean's and Koka's way); a shared cell's count is never 1, so it is copied, not written in place. An item that fails on another thread (an abort, a stop, an Int32 overflow) runs again on the calling thread in its turn, so the map fails at the last item that does, as in order; a table or box in reach, or a budget, keeps the map on one thread. Example: examples/mandelbrot-rows native, width 1200 with 5000 steps, 2.8 s on one thread and 0.75 s on four, the same picture, ThreadSanitizer clean; compiler/test/c-threads.test.ts (a big Int and a list shared by the items; aborts on other threads). Left: C201 (together on threads in C) | #2171 |

Example: compiler/test/linear.test.ts's turn (a rotation by π/6) of a shear, and a lift of both, are one matrix product with no call left, giving the evaluator's answers to the last bits; with NO_LINEAR=1 a call stays. No gallery example calls one linear function on another yet. WGSL's own (C30c) was kept by wgsl.ts | #2153 |

Example: primes 20000 48 to 42 ms with the same output (median of 15); compiler/test/owned-records.test.ts (rebuilt and passed on, a field read after, a helper reading it after, a closure) prints what the evaluator prints | #2132 |

| C186 | Map and Priority queue are weight-balanced trees in compiled JavaScript where the program's Map is: the chooser's tree for Map now reaches a library type that holds one, Priority queue (a Map keyed by priority and order), its field and every definition making or matching one rewritten with the program's (represent.ts `holders`); before, the check failed on Priority queue's definitions and the whole program kept the sorted entry lists. examples/agent's coupled search moves to its 0.5 m grid (5,217 and 5,308 states): its page starts in 0.8 s, from 10 s, the same answers. Two fixes made it run: arith.ts no longer resolves a definition at its default type when that type has no implementation of an interface it needs (`Float64 has no Collider`), and the core IR keeps the ways of a kept implementation's interface (core-js.ts `reachedImplementing`), so a field left to its ways compiles at all (C137's gap: polar, ways and fixed tests now compare compiled with evaluated). Example: `lab run examples/impact` prints "a 5 cm ball at 80 m/s touches at 0.0619 s, in 4 steps; the stick touches at 0.0611 s, in 11 steps", the same compiled, `lab test --compiled` | #2023 |

Through the core IR on JavaScript: queens counting all 2,680 boards of 11 (0.77 s) spends 12% in `number.range` building the lists its fold and `one of` walk, the rest in the search; calc on 20,000 lines spends about 1% (9 ms of 761) in `keep`'s second pass over the tokens, its fold already running on the line's text with no list of characters, and most in printing. Example: those two profiles, written down here and in NUMBERS.md step 5; a row again when a program measures the need | #2020 |
| L120b | Time of impact, shape against shape: examples/impact also throws the stick at a plank 2 m tall, 5 cm thick, turning 1 rad/s about its middle, both bodies on their own frame's motor. The gap is `between`, the distance between two convex polygons (0 when the separating axis theorem finds no edge with the other wholly outside it, else the nearest corner to an edge), and the bound the sum of |φ|·reach for each. Example: `lab run examples/impact` prints that at 60 m/s a step a frame finds them overlapping at frame 5 and advancement touching at 0.0825 s, and at 80 m/s the step never does while advancement finds them touching at 0.0607 s in 11 steps; the same as a JavaScript prototype, and `lab test --compiled` keeps it | #2019 |
| L120a | Time of impact for a rotating shape, step a: examples/impact throws a 40 cm stick, spinning, at a 5 cm wall and finds when it first touches by conservative advancement (Mirtich, 1996) along each frame's motor in 2D projective geometric algebra (Cl(2, 0, 1) from `clifford algebra`): a frame's slide and turn as one turn about `screw centre` (Chasles), its corners moved by e^(−sφ/2 c) through `clifford sandwich`, and each advance the gap over |φ|·reach. Beside it, a step a frame. Example: `lab run examples/impact` prints that at 60 m/s the step finds it in the wall at frame 5 (0.0833 s) and advancement at 0.0826 s, and that at 80 m/s the step never finds it while advancement finds it touching at 0.0611 s in 11 steps; the same as a JavaScript prototype, and `lab test` keeps it, compiled too | #2018 |
| L124 | A rapidity speed cap beside linear drag: examples/agent's page has two chasers following the blue agent, thrusting at it as hard as they can (2 m/s²), with a top speed of 3 m/s held by drag (`drag step`, moved exactly) or by rapidity: velocity a boost in Cl(2, 1) (`Plane time`, `clifford algebra` from squares −1, 1, 1), each frame's thrust a boost e^(½ dφ d e0) multiplied on its right, dφ = a·dt/(γc) (`rocket step`; Hestenes' boosts as rotors, Robb's rapidity). Also: grades.ts read a program's own definitions again on every path to its depth, exponential for definitions calling each other in several arms (the funnel's pull, pull left, restart), so `lab check` hung once the program had an algebra; their readings are now kept within one reading (5 s). Example: the page shows "Chasers: with drag (green) 2.46 m/s, with a speed cap (purple) 2.56 m/s, γ 1.928", γ matching 1/√(1 − (v/c)²), and the purple one overshoots the goal where the green one stops, seen in Chromium | #2015 |
| L123 | An agent with inertia, step 2, coupled: examples/agent's page has a Hybrid A*-style search (Dolgov, Thrun, Montemerlo and Diebel, 2010) over place and velocity beside L122's path-then-speed. Each step holds one of nine thrusts for 0.5 s, moved exactly under linear drag (`moved`), checked on the mesh four times along it; one state for each cell of place (1 m), speed and heading (`cell of`); the straight line at top speed as the estimate (L122's path length measured first in a prototype: no fewer states on this map). Both run once when the page starts (0.8 s compiled), and the frame's seconds are capped at 0.1 s so a slow frame doesn't jump. Example: the page draws both ways and says "5.79 s" against "6.00 s, 1603 states looked at" and "6.02 s" against "6.00 s, 1664 states looked at". The slow Map and Priority queue it found is C186 | #2010 |
| L122c | An agent with inertia, step 1c (L122 done): examples/agent's agent follows its path on a page. Each leg now holds where it goes, as a quadratic Bézier curve (start, bend, end; an arc's bend is the corner), `along` gives how far into a leg it is after a time (speeding up, holding, braking), and `place at` walks the legs to the agent's place at any time. main is a page: cells for the clock and the trip, a frame handler adding the frame's seconds, an svg of the triangles, the pillar and its hole (the pillar grown by the agent's radius, configuration space as Lozano-Pérez and Wesley's, 1979), the way and the agent, and the trip's line and credits. Example: `lab build-web examples/agent` gives a page whose agent drives (1, 2) to (8, 9) round the pillar in 5.79 s, rests a second, and drives (9, 1) to (2, 8), seen in Chromium | this PR |
| L122b | An agent with inertia, step 1b: examples/agent drives its path. Each corner is rounded by an arc of radius 0.5 m tangent to both legs (`legs`: r·tan(θ/2) cut off each, the arc's top speed √(a·r)), and the fastest speed along it comes from a forward pass and a backward pass (a fold from the end) over the legs (Bobrow, Dubowsky and Gibson; Shin and McKay, 1985), at rest at both ends, top speed 3 m/s, acceleration 2 m/s². main also prints a credits line (A*, Demyen and Buro, Lee and Preparata's funnel as Mononen wrote it, the speed profile). Example: `lab run examples/agent` prints "… 10.30 m in 5.79 s" and "… 10.77 m in 6.02 s", the same as a hand-written prototype, compiled and on the evaluator | #2006 |
| L122a | An agent with inertia, step 1a: examples/agent finds the shortest path across a room with a pillar on a hand-made navigation mesh of eight triangles: A* (Hart, Nilsson and Raphael) over the triangles, from centre to centre through shared edges, with the straight line to the goal as its estimate, and the funnel (adapted from Mikko Mononen's "simple stupid funnel algorithm") pulling the path tight round the corners. The program was built through the DSL (builder in project files, agent/build.ts); credited in its comments, README and CREDITS.md. Example: `lab run examples/agent` prints (1, 2) → (4, 7) → (8, 9), 10.30 m and (9, 1) → (4, 3) → (2, 8), 10.77 m, compiled and on the evaluator, and `lab test` keeps it | #2005 |
| L17 | Data-type upgrades (SHARING.md §2): when a module's type changes its cases or fields, its author publishes a migration with it, an offered function from the old version to the new of the same lineage (found by its pin, modules.ts `migrationsIn`; nothing new to write). `lab upgrade` moves a program on the old type through it first (`migrateType`): each value the program builds is built as before and passed through the migration, each field it reads is the new field of the same name, each case it matches the new case when its fields are the same, and then the functions over the type, their old pins read at the new type. What can't move (a field gone, a match on a case whose fields changed, a constructor passed as a value) is said and nothing moves; a type with no migration stays. Example: modules.test.ts's L17 test: Point gains `z`, with "point from version 1"; Bob's main printing norm and x of point(3, 4) on version 1 moves to version 2 and prints [25, 3]. Path namespaces, the row's other half, are a name layer needing nothing (SHARING.md §5) | #2002 |
| L114 | Lineage logs, the rest: code fetched by hash from any host (SHARING.md §4). `lab fetch <program> <url or path>` takes a module from wherever it is into the program's modules folder, checks every definition by its hash (verify) and its log, fetched beside it, against the authors the program's store pinned, and refuses either when it doesn't check out. The URL is the user's, not yet the log's. Example: Bob's program, its modules folder deleted, fetches Alice's sorting module and log from a path and runs; a copy with one node changed is refused ("insert was changed after it was published"), and Mallory's log is refused. Checked by hand with paths; the http(s) branch is Node's fetch, untried here (no local server allowed in this container). A browser over logs is a row if it earns one | #2001 |
| L114a | Lineage logs (SHARING.md §4): a module file's log beside it (`sorting.log.json`, cli/logs.ts), append-only, each entry signed with its author's Ed25519 key and holding the hash of the one before. `lab publish` signs each new version in as "current" (`--key file`, default ~/.lab/key.pem, made on first use); `lab broken <module.json> <name> <n> <reason>` marks a version broken. Only the key that wrote a lineage's first entry writes to it after, and a program's store pins that key (`authors`) the first time it takes the lineage, so a log rewritten whole under another key is refused. `lab upgrade` warns when a version the program uses is marked broken and never moves to a broken one. Example: Alice marks sort version 1 broken; Bob's upgrade warns "sort is marked broken by its author: drops duplicates" and moves him to version 2; Mallory's log for the same module is refused ("signed by another key than its author's"). test/lineage-log.test.ts: a changed, dropped or foreign entry is refused | #1999 |
| L115 | The project store (SHARING.md §4b): a program folder's store.json keeps every published definition it has used from its modules, by hash, with each lineage's versions it has had (modules.ts `addToStore`), filled by default by every `lab upgrade` and `lab downgrade`, before and after. It loads like a module (load.ts `programStore`), so the program opens and runs when a module's file is gone. `lab downgrade <program> <name>` moves it back to the version before (`previousVersion`) under the same pin rule, with that version's names, kept only if its tests pass. Example: a program upgraded from `order` version 2 to 3 (the same pin, sorting the other way) prints [3, 2, 1], and after its modules folder is deleted, `lab downgrade` takes it back to version 2 from the store, printing [1, 2, 3]; modules.test.ts's L115 test runs a program from its store with the module gone, and back | #1998 |
| L112 | Names pinned per program (SHARING.md §5). After `lab upgrade` a program keeps its own copy of the names it took (modules.ts `upgradeNames`): a name it gave itself stays, the module author's renames are said in one line for the whole upgrade and kept as they were, and `lab upgrade --take-names` takes the author's. Parameters go by place. Example: examples/sort published, its `sort` renamed `order` and published again; a program on version 1 upgraded keeps calling it `sort` and is told "the author renamed sort → order"; with --take-names it shows `order` (modules.test.ts's L112 test has the same with `square`) | #1997 |
| L111 | Upgrades by pin generality (SHARING.md §2). A new version takes an old one's place when its pin is at least as general (signatures.ts `atLeastAsGeneral`: the new type, instantiated, unifies with the old held rigid, no new effects, no interface the old didn't say), so `upgrade` no longer needs equal pins. A program folder's `modules` folder holds modules beyond the library, checked on load (load.ts `programModules`). `lab upgrade <program>` moves it onto the newest version of each module definition it uses that fits (modules.ts `upgradeOwn`), keeping it only if it still checks and its tests pass (T7); a narrower one is kept back, with both types. Publishing a version again after a newer one makes it the newest again. The editor's half is E254. Example: a program on version 1 of a module's `sort` (List Float64) moves to version 2 (List a, where a has <) and prints the same; version 3 back at Float64 is refused; version 4 sorting the other way fails main's accepted answer and nothing is written (modules.test.ts's L111 test has the first two) | #1996 |
| L110 | Publishing keeps the draft id as the lineage id (SHARING.md §1). `lab publish <program> <name…> [-o module.json]` (modules.ts `publishVersion`) publishes the named definitions and the drafts they use as their next versions in a module file: a module's `lineages` list each lineage's versions oldest first, the old ones stay in it (published code is never deleted), only the newest is offered, and publishing what didn't change adds nothing. `replaces`, which nothing wrote, is gone: what replaces what is read from the lineages (`replacing`). A program's ref is the exact version, its `@` id, and its lineage is found from the module (`lineageOf`). Example: modules.test.ts's "publishing a draft again after an edit adds a version to its lineage", and examples/sort's `sort` published, its `insert` edited and published again (version 2 of both, version 1 kept) | #1994 |
| L109 | std published with lineages and versions, and hashes that carry their recipe (SHARING.md §3). std's ids are its lineage ids; library/std-versions.json (kept by library/build.ts, modules.ts `addVersions`) lists every version each std definition has had, oldest first, so a change to a declaration adds one and keeps the old. Each module records the version of every std definition its code refers to (`std`, modules.ts `baseVersions`), and verify says "html was published against another version of std's std.attribute: publish it again" instead of that its code changed. An id says its recipe (hash.ts `recipeOf`): today's is the unlettered one, a later one a letter w to z after the `@`, and verify refuses an id of a recipe it doesn't know as that. Also: build.ts now records the cases module's moved definitions in moved.json, and cases.json was stale on main (rebuilt). Example: modules.test.ts's "a change to a std declaration adds a version and keeps the old" | #1992 |

## Demos

| id | item | PR |
|---|---|---|
| D9 | Compass: a perpendicular from a circle's own centre, or an angle bisector with a side point on the corner, drew a line pointing wherever rounding said, swinging as points moved (Ersagun 2026-11-02). Its vector is 0; such a line is now nothing, and the help says so | #1098 |
| D8 | Compass: an Angle bisector tool, three clicks (a point on one side, the corner, a point on the other). Each side as a line of size 1 running from the corner, l / √(l · l); their sum halves the angle. Test: two bisectors of a triangle cross at a point equally far from all three sides | #1092 |
| D7 | Compass: a Perpendicular tool. Click a point, then a line: (p ∧ s ∧ ∞)*, the line through p meeting s at right angles (dot 0 with p, s and ∞); with a circle it is the line through the point and the centre | #1090 |
| D6 | Compass: a Midpoint tool (Ersagun 2026-11-02, after building an incircle by hand). Two points p and q of weight 1: p − q is their perpendicular bisector (x · p = x · q), and the midpoint is where it crosses the line through them. Perpendicular and angle bisector follow as D7, D8 | #1088 |
| I147 | Compass: a circle round a point through a point where two lines cross missed that point, and shrank as the crossing moved away from the origin (Ersagun 2026-11-02, building an incircle). Two lines' crossing was read off the flat point as o ⌋ T = x − ½\|x\|² ∞, which has the point's x and y but isn't a point (it doesn't square to 0), so c · p was off by ½\|x\|². The crossing is made again from its x and y; test/compass.test.ts | #1079 |
| D5 | The guitar pedal restyled as a stompbox (Ersagun 2026-10-30): the converter's colours with a dark version; the two sources light up while they play (the page's `data-playing`); the controls are the pedal's dark top, its switch lit orange while driven (a class worked out from `driven`), the drive in a monospace readout; the waveform a scope screen with a grid. A `styles` definition of rules and every element named by its class | this PR |
| D4 | Pig restyled in the converter's look (Ersagun 2026-10-30): its colours with a dark version, Play a game the filled button, the note on the handlers a soft card, the game's lines a card with the last line (who won) bold; the empty log and tally take no room before a game. A `styles` definition of rules and every element named by its class, no inline styles left | this PR |
| D3 | Two dice restyled in the converter's look (Ersagun 2026-10-30: the HTML demos look too primitive): the converter's colours with a dark version, a filled Roll button, the chart a card with rounded green bars on a track and the red tick for fair dice. A `styles` definition of rules and every element named by its class; the only inline styles left are a bar's width and a tick's place, which are computed. The unit converter (D2) already had its own style sheet and this look, so it is unchanged | this PR |
| D1 | The nested to-do restyled (Ersagun 2026-10-30: long named buttons on each item, the tick a separate button, add a step taking the top field's title). The tick is the round box before the title; ↑, + and × are small buttons that show on hover; + opens a field under the item for the step's title (Enter adds, Escape closes); a count and a bar at the top. The look is one `styles` definition of rules, every element named by its class, so the code has no inline styles. html.ts focuses an `autofocus` element mounted after the page loaded. Also closes I63 (the buttons wrapping onto 2-3 lines, QA10 L6) | this PR |

| C63e | A bug in the one-holder check (one-holder.ts): the same nodes walked twice, as routes' `near` does with its fold (a match's scrutinee, and its otherwise again), counted the second walk's fold result as used after it was changed, so routes' sets failed. Tests: ters/test/one-holder.test.ts | #910 |

The chooser takes it where a program changes items by position in a loop; `List as written` now costs `replace at` too, and the list boundary for builtins goes back through the row's own nil and cons when it isn't the array (library-reps.ts). Binary, where RRB (the row's name) is 32-wide: same costs. Tests: rep-laws (its 8 jobs agree through items), rep-costs (each grows as its row says), library-reps (chosen, and the same answers in the evaluator, WebAssembly and C) | #912 |
| C70 | The chooser keeps the trees when a map only passes through a generic definition (GAPS.md round 6 N1): library-reps.ts `generic` now counts a call only where the callee looks at the value, i.e. its type variable reaches show, equal or compare there or in what it hands it to (`looked`, the reading of compile.ts's M4 `compared`); `map`, `first`, `keep` and `sort by` pass items on unseen. notebook at 3000 notes: compiled 235× → 9× the ideal (trees), 7× with the set's table; 300 notes 32× → 6×. Test: library-reps.test.ts (a set in pairs mapped over keeps the tree, prints the same; one shown through a helper still keeps the definition) | #926 |
| C40 (main's waits) | Compiled JavaScript answers what main leaves unhandled of Await, as the evaluator's runtime does (L71c): the runtime's `$main` drives main's generator, `start timer(ms)` gives a ticket ready at the world's clock plus ms, and awaiting a ticket, a timer or `any of(…)` waits until the least ready time and goes on with its value. The world's `wait` sleeps; a world with none gets a promise of main's value, resumed by setTimeout. `lab compile`'s program uses it, so a compiled main that waits runs at all (before, its generator was never started). test/await.test.ts: together, race and within compiled give the evaluator's prints and waits | #958 |
| C73 | Checked, put back: `take(sort by(xs, key), n)` as a selection (keep the best n in a sorted list of at most n, each item put in front of the first kept one whose key isn't before its own, so ties come out as the stable sort leaves them). It gave the sort's answers in a test, but notebook never writes that shape where it counts (`hits text` takes 5 of a list sorted in `ranked`, and uses its own Int `take`), so reading it there needs inlining across definitions for a few percent; not kept (GAPS.md round 6 N5) | #957 |
| C72 (hash-table side) | std.ts's `Table` keeps each version's sorted entries (a set's items) the first time `entries of` asks: a version never changes, so a map read many times, as notebook's postings are, is sorted once. Notebook's table run at 3000 notes: 700 ms to 510 (median of 21). test/table.test.ts checks every listed version against a copy per version. The trees' side (walking in order) stays on C72's row | #947 |
| C71 | The hash table without one holder per map (GAPS.md round 6 N2), another way than the row's per-value choice: every version of std.ts's `Table` stays a value. A put or remove writes into the one JS Map and turns the version it was given into the change that undoes it; reading an old version undoes the changes between (Baker's rerooting; Conchon and Filliâtre). So library-reps.ts no longer asks one-holder.ts, only that keys are Int or Text. notebook at 3000 notes compiled: Map now a hash table too, 8× → 6× the ideal; routes unchanged within noise. Tests: ters/test/table.test.ts (4000 random changes and reads over branching versions agree with a copy per version), compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts (a map and a set whose old version is read again get the table and print the same) | #931 |
